PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
____________________________________________________

INFORMATION NOTE
What this Part is about: This Part describes how judgments and Court
orders are prepared and how they can be corrected, varied or set aside,
and contains rules applying to civil enforcement agencies. This Part also
includes rules for how abandoned goods are to be dealt with and deals
with how judgments and orders from jurisdictions outside Alberta may be
registered in the Court.
Words and phrases used in this Part that have defined meanings in the
Appendix [Definitions] include: applications judge; assessment officer;
civil enforcement agency; claim; commencement document; Court;
court clerk; defendant; enactment; encumbrance; file; foreclosure action;
foreclosure order; judge; judgment; judgment creditor; judgment debtor;
land; lawyer; order; partnership; party; personal representative; plaintiff;
prescribed form; procedural order; property; record; redemption order;
remedy; rules; secured land; secured property; trustee
____________________________________________________
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
____________________________________________________

9-3
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.1(1)
DIVISION 1
PREPARATION AND ENTRY OF
JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Form of Judgments and Orders
9.1(1) Judgments and orders must be divided into consecutively numbered
paragraphs.¹
(2) Every judgment and order must include
(a) the date on which and the location at which it was pronounced,
(b) the name of the applications judge or judge who made it, and
(c) the date of entry.²
[Alta. Reg.124/10;143/11;136/22]

Related Provisions
3.35 (consent judgments) ; 4.23 (security for costs order) ; 6.4 (ex parte applications and

injunctions); 9.5 (entry of judgments and orders) ; 12.39 (fiats) ; 13.38 (fiats) ; 13.45 (giving
clerk notice of order directing clerk to act).
A. Cross-References
On ﬁats, see Rr.1.4(3) and 12.39. On how to word a formal order or judgment, see R.9.2n.
On dating an order, see R.9.6n.
B. Judgments and Orders Generally
1. Categories
On this topic generally, the traditional English book of precedents and commentary was Seton.³
A judgment is a type of court order, which ﬁnally settles some rights. A judgment decides
the main issue in the action, whereas orders decide procedural and collateral issues?⁴
There
is no judgment until the exact amount claimed is stated by the court; an approximation or close
estimate is not enough.⁵
A decision that evidence is inadmissible is not a “judgment”.⁶
Nor is an order for interim spousal
support, which can be varied.⁷
Nor is review of a lawyer’s account to her client at the lawyer’s
instance?⁸
But a declaration can be a judgment for purposes of the Law of Property Act.⁹

1944 C. R.365. Cf. Judic. Ord. r.467 and Eng.1883 O.52 r.13.
3 Seton, Forms of Judgments and Orders in the High Court (7th ed. Stevens 1912).
4 Thompson v. Alta. Racing Comm. (1997) 210 AR 272, 56 Alta LR(3d) 387. A judgment decides the parties’
rights or a controversy with ﬁnality, whereas an order generally is made during the progress of a matter to
settle a procedural question or a collateral issue: Alta. Turkey Producers v. Leth (#2) 2006 ABQB 283, 399
AR 259.
5 Remington Dev. Corp. v. C. P. R. (#2) 2023 ABKB 493, JCC 0801 03152 (Aug 30) (¶’s 108-21).
6 R. v. Kelly 2001 SCC 25, [2001] 1 SCR 741, appeal quashed from 122 OAC 201, 135 CCC(3d) 449.
7 P. M. M. v. R. W. M. (#2) 2000 ABQB 624, 279 AR 119.

9 Oscar Fech Constr. v.509211 Alta. (1994) 160 AR 334, 24 Alta LR(3d) 295.

9-4
R.9.1(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Sometimes it is not easy to tell whether a court document is a judgment or a mere order, and
names can be misleading. For example, an order nisi of foreclosure is a true judgment.¹
Many orders which are not judgments can be varied or revoked later, but judgments cannot.
Once a formal judgment is granted after notice and then entered, it can be set aside or varied
only by an appeal to an appellate court²
(or for clearly proven fraud). Subject to that, it is ﬁnal
and perpetual.³
It cannot even be attacked collaterally in other legal proceedings,⁴
e. g. , during a
contempt proceeding to enforce it. Even if it is alleged to be based on an unconstitutional law.⁵
Unless maybe its face shows lack of jurisdiction.⁶
Even if there is a right to have a judgment set
aside (on appeal, or because it was given ex parte), it is valid until that is done.⁷
However, some exceptions to this ﬁnality rule are found in Rr.9.12 to 9.16, and the notes to
them.
The common law conﬁned judgments to speaking once for all, e. g. to awarding one lump
sum as damages. Periodic payments could be awarded only by consent, as part of a settlement.
But now monetary awards may be given in instalments or as annuities or other structures, even
without consent.⁸
Orders by non-courts, or lower courts, often can be approved by the Court of King’s Bench
or ﬁled with it, and can then be enforced as a Court of King’s Bench order or judgment is. If an
arbitration award is so treated, disobeying it is not contempt of court?⁹
2. Interpretation
A court order is interpreted in light of the pleadings, its own language, and the circumstances
when it was granted.¹⁰
A court order must be interpreted as a whole, in the context of the pleadings, arguments,
factual and legal context, and the intent of the court giving the order.¹¹
Even if the order derived
from a “template order”.¹²
But what governs are the express terms of an order, not comments
which the judge made during argument.¹³

9-5
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.1(2)
How to interpret a previous court order does not depend upon the subjective views of any of
the parties.¹
Even a consent order is by the court, which determines its meaning in light of the
pleadings, its language, and the evidence and circumstances at the time it was made. Only at the
end, if there is still ambiguity, should one consider extrinsic evidence.²
One should also consider the central objectives of efﬁciency, ﬁnality, and (when enforcing an
arbitral award) respect for the forum of arbitration. The latter includes conﬁdentiality, privacy, party
autonomy, expertise of the arbitrator, procedural ﬂexibility, and economy.³
The mere fact that a decree of divorce incorporates terms of a settlement agreement into it
does not necessarily mean that the judge has mentally approved all of them or in effect ordered
them.⁴
Even after the form of judgment is signed, the court keeps power to decide its effect.⁵
The danger of possible overpayment by being paid twice, does not make two possible
remedies for a plaintiff inconsistent and require an election at the time judgment is given. The
remedy instead is later to require some refund (or enforce other contractual obligations) if there is
overpayment (or full payment).⁶
Injunctions must be clear about what is or is not to be done. And the party accused of contempt
must have had actual knowledge, and have intentionally done or omitted the thing forbidden or
ordered. To whom the order applies must be clear. Interpreting a court order is a legal question
reviewed on appeal for correctness. The parties do not set its meaning.⁷
A verdict (of assessors) not followed by any judgment is a nullity?⁸
The party who applied for and got an order must accept all of it, and cannot eat its cake and
have it too.⁹
C. Injunctions After Trial
On interpreting injunctions, see Part B above.
On injunctions before trial, see R.6.4n. It is vital to see if the proposed injunction is interlocutory
or ﬁnal, because the tests for the two are signiﬁcantly different.¹⁰

9-6
R.9.1(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Perpetual injunctions¹
are part of a ﬁnal judgment, and usually given after a trial, though in
theory they might be given on a motion for judgment, such as a summary judgment motion.²
There is no right to a transition period, and the court has power to make the perpetual injunction
effective at once.³
Perpetual injunctions are either negative (forbidding something),⁴
or mandatory
(ordering that something be done).⁵
New arguments are not a ground to reconsider such an injunction.⁶
There should not be an injunction after trial where damages would be an adequate remedy,
and the plaintiff delayed in suing.⁷
Where a statute authorizes an injunction to enforce the statute, the court has very narrow scope
to rely on laches or equitable discretion to withhold the injunction.⁸
But absent such legislation, an
injunction is an equitable remedy and the court has discretion to refuse it, e. g. for unclean hands
or failure of the plaintiff to do equity.⁹
For a discussion of quia timet injunctions, see the Simonelli case.¹⁰
Since breaching an injunction can have serious consequences, what it prohibits must be
clearly stated, so its subject can know exactly what he or she can or cannot do.¹¹
The importance
of clarity and precision in injunctions is again stressed, to avoid unfairness or ongoing disputes.¹²
Historically, courts have been reluctant to order post-trial activity which the court would have
to supervise, but that might have to be done to enforce the duty to consult with aboriginals.¹³
And
when ﬁnal injunctions are not issued against the Crown, it is possible that interlocutory ones might
be.¹⁴
A permanent injunction against posting internet defamation of the plaintiff and allowing a
warrant from a judge for arrest by proof by afﬁdavit of breaches, with a hearing to follow, was
given. The defendant’s conduct throughout showed that nothing else would be effective.¹⁵

9-7
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.1(2)
At common law, an injunction against the Crown was impossible, and s.17(1) of the
Proceedings Against the Crown Act of Alberta substitutes a declaration. There are exceptions,
allowing injunctions against unconstitutional Crown actions, or to prevent communication of an
ultra vires or illegal act. The authorities conﬂict somewhat (and they are reviewed at length).¹
Traditionally, courts do not issue coercive judgments against the Crown, because of a tradition
that the government will respect declaratory orders, but that constitutional protocol can continue
viably only if the government does in fact comply with declaratory orders.²
It is doubtful that a court can order an annoying or repetitive person not to communicate with
a Cabinet Minister or other public ﬁgure.³
D. Miscellaneous
A judge has no right to refuse to decide an issue properly put before him or her, as long as the
matters involved are more than trivial and the question is not moot.⁴
Doubt is not a bar, and in civil
matters if evidence conﬂicts, the judge is to ﬁnd the more probable fact.
It is customary to number only the operative paragraphs of an order or judgment, not the
recitals.
The Charter does not apply to court orders?⁵
A formal judgment, instead of naming a sum of money, can adopt and incorporate by reference
a speciﬁc formula for computing it.⁶
One cannot get judgment against a non-party, e. g. a mere “third party by order” under the
Insurance Act.⁷
Sometimes issuing a joint trial judgment respecting two different lawsuits between
different sets of people with similar issues is not a good idea if privacy would be invaded. However
it seemed to be more efﬁcient in one case.⁸
Such joint reasons for judgment are common.
It is awkward to have reasons for judgment where a large number of people are identiﬁed only
by initials. Real names, not initials, should be used in a formal order, as those orders do not go
on any public website.⁹
The law is reviewed about when taking judgment against one defendant bars going on with the
suit against other defendants.¹⁰
At a case management hearing, if the judge says that he will consider a draft order, and if
appropriate grant it or something like it, that is not a ﬁnal decision, nor pronouncement. The other
side did not have notice of a decision until he got a copy of the ﬁled order, which is when time to
appeal started.¹¹

9-8
R.9.1(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
A matrimonial property order ﬁled in the Land Titles Ofﬁce against land effectively charged it
and made the payee a secured creditor on a later bankruptcy.¹
If a trial judgment criticizes the appellant in a way that he thinks unfair, and he wants those
passages removed, an appeal court (still less one justice of it) cannot remove them; they are
not part of what the court ordered. The trial judge can be asked by the appellant to issue a
corrigendum if the trial judge thinks the criticisms go too far.²
Where a statute lets a decision of a tribunal be ﬁled in King’s Bench and be enforced, that
allows all the usual enforcement remedies, including civil contempt, but it is not a court order or
judgment.³
If a lawyer attended or participated in a motion or case conference, he or she has a duty to
obey anything which the resulting order directs, even if he or she has since ceased to be retained.⁴
Breach of a Rule is not itself contempt, if there is no judge’s order compelling obedience. The
court can often relieve against not obeying a Rule, especially if no prejudice results. If the winner
of a motion does not promptly draft the formal order, the losing party has remedies under Rr.9.2
and 9.3.⁵
Some vexatious litigants of the pseudolegal sort annotate existing court orders and send them
back to the court. It is part of trying to foist legal promises to pay, upon other people. That is legally
ineffective. These are strong indicia that the person is vexatious.⁶
E. Currency and Interest
See also R.9.9n. On interest generally, see R.3.39 n. B.
Even if accounts or a claim are in foreign funds, after a Canadian suit, the judgment must be
in Canadian money.⁷
If foreign currency underlies the claim, the task of the court is to select the most fair and
equitable exchange rate date. It has to be either breach date, or judgment date. The better date is
that of the judgment.⁸
Where no evidence was given to the trial judge of the difference, and he or
she only has one date, then that is the one which has to be used.⁹
One must not confuse pre-judgment interest with the date for converting a foreign currency
into Canadian dollars in a Canadian judgment; the two do not duplicate each other. The court is

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.2(1)
to select the fairest exchange date, and if the equities are equal, the wrongdoer should bear the
burden.¹
In one case the date chosen was the date of the appellate judgment, not the date of the
tortious taking.²
The Judgment Interest Act deﬁnes “judgment debt” as a sum made payable by or under a
judgment in a civil proceeding, which is not true of a compensation order by a tribunal. And the
more speciﬁc provisions under the Surface Rights Act take precedence over the more general
provisions of the Judgment Interest Act. So the amount would be the statutory rate applied to the
modiﬁed compensation substituted by the court.³
Preparation of Judgments and Orders
9.2(1) The Court may direct which party is to prepare a draft of the judgment or
order pronounced by the Court, but if the Court does not do so, the successful
party is responsible for preparing the draft.
(2) The following rules apply, unless the Court otherwise orders:
(a) within 10 days after the judgment or order is pronounced, the
responsible party must prepare a draft of the judgment or order in
accordance with the Court’s pronouncement and serve it on every
party in attendance at the hearing, but if the responsible party does
not prepare and serve the draft then any other party may do so;
(b) within 10 days after the draft judgment or order is served, each party
served may
(i) approve the draft, or
(ii) object to the draft, providing particulars of the objection;
(c) if a party does not approve or object to the draft judgment or order
within the 10 days described in clause (b) but all other requirements
are met and service of the draft is proved, the judgment or order may
be signed and entered. [Alta. Reg.124/10;122/12]
Information Note
Rule 9.5(2) [Entry of judgments and orders] requires judgments and orders to be entered
within 3 months after pronouncement unless the Court otherwise permits.

judgments); 9.35 (foreclosure orders) ; 13.38 (fiats) ; 14.77 (judgments after appeal)

9-10
R.9.2(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
A. Approval by Opposing Party
These 2010 Rules impose new timelines for the preparation of draft judgments and orders, for
the entry of judgments and orders, and a mechanism for entry where the opposing party does not
approve or object to a draft served upon him or her.
A lawyer who appears in court and later ceases to act has a continuing obligation to draft or
approve the resulting order, even if the lawyer has been ﬁred by the client. A client cannot give
instructions to a lawyer not to approve a properly drafted order, and “not having instructions” is no
excuse for the lawyer.¹
If the one lawyer before decision can foresee victory, he or she should bring to court a draft
formal judgment (or order) (or several possible versions) in his or her favour, ready for the justice
or Applications Judge to sign on the spot. Where that is not done, the process of later entering
the formal judgment or order previously (i. e. , drafting it and having it signed and ﬁled) before 2010
was often unnecessarily slow and frustrating. That is addressed by the timelines and procedure
in the 2010 Rules. If not for this Rule, the Clerk or applications judge or judge should not usually
sign it later without either a further hearing or a signed endorsement by the opposing lawyer.²
That
signature often takes months to secure. That is not proper.
Whether the draft formal order is prepared promptly or late, the party who lost the application
should not resist approving the draft formal order because that part disagrees with the decision
which the justice or applications judge pronounced and which the formal order records; the
remedy for such disagreement is an appeal.³
It is wrong for a party not to respond to a draft formal order ﬂowing from a decision made by
the court.⁴
If the opposing party has no lawyer or the matter is very simple, the winning lawyer
may ask the justice or applications judge at the time of pronouncement to dispense with the need
for endorsement under R.9.4(2) (c). In other cases, if the losing lawyer is thought to be slow or
difﬁcult, the winning lawyer could do more than merely draft a formal judgment (or order) and ask
the losing lawyer to approve its wording; the winner could also ﬁle an application under R.9.3 to
settle the minutes of the judgment (or order) and serve it along with the draft. That way the whole
thing should be accomplished in a few days while everyone’s memory is fresh. If the judge who
gave the order is sitting out of town, a telephone application might be arranged.⁵
“Approved as to the order granted”, on a formal order, is ambiguous: is it consent, or approval
of the form of what the judge has earlier entered?⁶
The distinctions in R.9.2 between having a proposed form of order approved and simply
having approval of the proposed form of order dispensed with, are explained.⁷

Edm 2303 0145 AC (Nov 24) (¶’s 11-14).

ABQB 151, 355 AR 67.

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.2(2)
No approval as to form of a formal order is necessary if the judge himself or herself signed it,
on notice. That he or she had no clerk’s notes present will not be presumed; evidence of that gap
is necessary.¹
Refusing to approve the formal order without saying why, was not notice of any particular ﬂaw,
nor did it create any rights or duties.²
A formal order was amended, signed and entered though it did not conform to the Applications
Judge’s pronounced decision, all without notice to the opposing counsel, going to a different
Applications Judge. This was achieved by misstatements to the court and disobeying the judge’s
procedural instructions. Heavy personal full indemnity costs were levied on the lawyer.³
B. Contents of Formal Judgments
The wording of a formal judgment is discussed in the C. P. E.⁴
A formal judgment should not try to summarize the judge’s reasons, nor record contingent
or obiter ﬁndings; it is for the result alone.⁵
A formal judgment should not include reasons or
procedural history.⁶
The Court of Appeal’s formal judgment is to record the ultimate outcome,
not the arguments, nor the reasons.⁷
If the trial court’s formal judgment set out three precise
questions and gave an answer to each, on appeal the Court of Appeal formal judgment should
follow the same format, stating the questions, and setting out the answers.⁸
Court of Appeal memoranda of judgment or reasons for judgment do not generally deal with
costs, given the default Rule, R.14.88.⁹
A formal judgment should show which defendants are
responsible to pay the damages, should reﬂect the multiplicity of claims by the plaintiff, should
say who is responsible for any costs, and specify the scale.¹⁰
But if oral reasons are not recorded,
see further below.
There are traditional contents for many types of formal judgment (or order), not all of which
terms the court recites when granting them; they are implied. Therefore, books of precedents of
judgments are important.¹¹

R.9.2(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
There is a discussion of difﬁculties in wording a formal judgment, including both the trial
judge’s decision on the main points and then an agreed reference to the experts to settle amounts
and details.¹
C. Reasons for Decision
Reasons for judgment have 4 functions. The ﬁrst is to tell the parties and others what the
formal judgment will say. Since a judgment is presumed to take effect at once on pronouncement,
that is important. The second function of reasons for judgment is to explain why the judgment was
given, which is important on appeal. The third is to show the parties, especially the one who is
losing, that they were heard and understood and that the process was fair. The fourth is to tell any
later judges (including an appeal court) why the decision was reached and precisely what was
then ordered.
Reasons for decision are desirable, but even if there is a duty to give them, their absence in
a civil case is generally not a free-standing ground of appeal.²
However, where the evidence (in a
criminal case) is confused and contradictory, a trial judge sitting alone should give reasons for his
or her conclusion.³
How much a judge must say in his or her reasons for decision depends upon
the issues before the court. Where it is a criminal trial and acquittal can be based simply on lack
of proof, then it may not take very much.⁴
Reasons, especially about costs, need not be detailed
if the judgment as a whole makes the reasons clear.⁵
That reasons of an Applications Judge or
justice do not mention some submission by a party, does not mean that it was ignored.⁶
Though inadequate reasons are not a stand-alone error of law (even on appeal from an
Applications Judge) nevertheless inadequate ones raise a question. That is whether the reasoning
arc was clear from the judgment and the evidence, or if the lack of reasons create a reasoned
belief that the justice or Applications Judge appealed from forgot, ignored or misconceived
material evidence.⁷
The justice deciding can announce a result with reasons to follow, whether for the whole suit,
or for one ruling, subject to two provisos. First, there should be no long delay before their issue,
and second, the face of the reasons should not appear crafted to answer any intervening appeal.⁸
If there is any chance of appeal, it is the duty of counsel to try to have oral reasons for
judgment recorded somehow.⁹
If they are not recorded, a few of their highlights may be recited in
the formal judgment, but those are usually very bare bones, and difﬁculties are caused where one
recites points of law in formal judgments.¹⁰

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.3
On correcting the transcript of a judge’s oral reasons, see R.9.12 n. B.
Where regulations give a municipal Appeal Board 15 days to give reasons for a decision, they
need not be a work of perfection. That the parties may have understood the issues, however, does
not justify “reasons” which are entirely conclusory.¹
Semble, discussions between the judge and counsel during argument are not part of the
reasons for decision, and what the judge says then is sometimes not ﬁnal, but one has to see
what is oral reasons.²
On cutting and pasting large parts of one party’s written argument into a court or tribunal’s
reasons for decision see a review of authority and decision.³
D. Miscellaneous
In acrimonious litigation where attempts to settle the wording of a formal order had failed,
though Canadian courts are reactive and not inquisitorial (as in Germany), “using this venue is
a privilege not a right”. So the Applications Judge gave directions to move the dispute forward,
effective at once and without need for a formal order.⁴
Dispute Over Contents of Judgment or Order
9.3 If there is a dispute about the contents of a judgment or order, the disputants
may apply to the Court to resolve the dispute.

Related Provisions
6.3 (applications) ; 9.4(3) (signing orders) ; 13.1 (any judge can act)

Settling the minutes of the order (i. e. the wording of the formal order or judgment) is not a
time to reargue the merits, still less to engage in debating irrelevancies, which irrelevant steps
may lead to personal costs.⁵
A motion to settle the minutes of a formal order or judgment is not a
rehearing of the original motion or trial, and the question on settling the terms is not whether the
judge was right the ﬁrst time.⁶
It is not fair for a party who lost to negotiate an altered formal order,
and then after that, to spring on the respondent an unexpected and late appeal.⁷
One judge can approve the form of an order granted by another judge.⁸
In one case, any mistakes by an impugned solicitor in drafting formal orders from chambers
were either reasonable, pardonable, slight negligence, or even non-existent (i. e. in some respects
the drafts may have been correct). It was a situation (no transcript, different judge, argument and
decision interwoven) making order-drafting difﬁcult.⁹
On calculating continuing damages and prejudgment interest, see Rr.3.39n and 9.9n.

R.9.4(1) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Signing Judgments and Orders
9.4(1) A judge or applications judge may sign a judgment or order when it is
pronounced.
(2) If a judge or an applications judge does not sign a judgment or order when
it is pronounced, the court clerk may sign the judgment or order in any of the
following circumstances:
(a) in a proceeding which a party adverse in interest did not attend;
(b) if the party adverse in interest approves the form of the judgment or
order or waives approval of its form;
(c) if the Court directs that approval of the form of the judgment or order
by a party is not required;
(d) if the Court directs the court clerk to sign the judgment or order.¹
(3) In any circumstance other than those described in subrule (2), a judgment or
order must be signed by a judge or applications judge.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;136/22]

Related Provisions
3.35 (approving consent orders) ; 9.3 (dispute over order) ; 13.1 (any judge can act)

In the “News & Announcements” part of the Court of King’s Bench website is a May 21, 2020
notice to the profession about filing signed desk orders.
Not every order in an action need be approved before entry by every party. Only parties
appearing in court can ever approve it, and the court can waive R.9.2, or direct the Clerk to sign
without approval.²
A lawyer has a professional duty to sign a proper formal order resulting from a motion or case
conference which he or she attended or participated in, even if he has ceased to be retained since
and cannot get instructions.³
If delay is likely or dangerous, a judge pronouncing an order or judgment may at the same time
ﬁx a future time and date to settle the wording of the formal order or judgment.⁴
An odd situation, including forgery of part of a formal order, is the Prefontaine case.⁵
The judge can still sign the formal order when the parties are in person and the prevailing
party has a form of order ready. Otherwise the prevailing party prepares the order and (assuming
R.9.4(2) (c) is not invoked) sends it to the opposing party for their review or approval. If they do
not respond within ten days the prevailing party may submit the order by email to the Court for
endorsement of the judge. The party should indicate in the email whether R.9.4(2) (c) was invoked,
or whether they provided the order to the opposing party and the ten-day period has elapsed.

4 IGM Res. Corp. v.979708 Alta.2004 ABQB 925, 364 AR 167. For example, if the judge is sitting in the same

9-15
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.5(1)
A formal judgment (or order) after contest should be prepared by the winning lawyer. If the
justice or applications judge does not sign it at the time he or she pronounces the decision, then
the formal judgment or order should be signed by the losing lawyer, whose name should also be
printed or stamped. Above the signature should be the words “approved as to form, ” or “approved
as being the judgment made. ” It should not say “consented to” if there was a contest. Nor should
it say “approved as to form and content, ” for that is ambiguous, and also seems to suggest a
consent judgment. It is implied that a signature approving a form of formal judgment may be
given in an electronic form, and the judgment need not recite that. If there are multiple parties,
counterparts may be signed. Only afﬁdavits need original manual signatures.¹
Before arguing a contested motion to settle the wording of a formal order, counsel should
secure a copy of the notes made at the time of pronouncement by the Clerk sitting in court.
At a judicial dispute resolution session, the parties believed that they had settled the lawsuit,
and said that there would be a consent order. It was drafted a few days later and sent to the other
side who said that it was by consent. The lawyer who had drafted the order sent it to the judge
saying “there has been no request for changes”. The judge signed it without getting a signature
from, or contacting, the other side. It should not have been signed in that fashion, as no order had
been “pronounced” by the court, and so the Rule on how to sign pronounced orders did not apply.
And a full explanation should have been given to the judge when he was asked to sign it. That was
in effect an ex parte application requiring full disclosure.²
If one counsel asks the court to invoke R.9.4(2) (c) (e. g. because the opponent has no lawyer),
then that counsel undertakes a duty to draft the order fairly and accurately, as the court and all
parties will later rely on it. That is an important duty. Where a counsel did not do that (omitting the
word “temporary” and an expiry date), thus misleading a later judge, the later order relying on that
error was set aside on appeal, after that counsel tried to blame the error on the Clerk, and that
counsel had to pay personally a lump sum in costs.³
Entry of Judgments and Orders
9.5(1) Subject to subrule (2), every judgment and every order is entered by ﬁling
it with the court clerk, who must make a note in the court ﬁle of the entry and the
date of entry.⁴
(2) A judgment or order is not to be entered more than 3 months after it is
pronounced except with the Court’s permission, which may only be obtained on
application and after notice is served on each of the other parties.⁵

2 Gingrich v. Gingrich 2012 ABCA 371, [2012] AR Uned 577 (Dec 6).
3 Svederus v. Engi 2019 ABCA 155, Calg 1801 0267 AC.

to 1944 C. R.375, which had origins in Eng.1883 O.41 r.1 and 1897 Ont. R.635-7, and was amended by
O. C.1042-46.

9-16
R.9.5(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Information Note
Rule 3.7 [Post-judgment transfer of action] permits a judgment creditor to apply to the Court,
on notice to each of the other parties, for a temporary transfer of the action to a different
judicial centre for purposes of an application to enforce the judgment or order.

Related Provisions
1.4(3) (recording directions of court) ; 13.5 (varying time) ; 13.38 (fiats) ; 13.45 (giving clerk

notice of order directing clerk to act); 14.78 (entry of judgment after appeal) ; 15.7 (transitional
rule).
Documents are now usually ﬁled electronically, not by attendance at the counter of the
Clerk’s ofﬁce, unless it is an urgent (same day/next day) Order signed in paper during a Regular
Chambers hearing. If it is the latter it may be ﬁled at the counter (urgent Counter in Edmonton or
Calgary).¹
It takes some weeks to get back a ﬁled (entered) formal order or judgment, so if there
is a ﬂaw or misunderstanding, the process can be slow.
The 2010 Rule reduces the deadline to enter a judgment or order from one year to three
months.
In some circumstances, exceeding the time limit could lead the court to forbid entry, but not
where there was no real prejudice, and any prejudice could have been resolved by the winner’s
entering the order.²
Rule 9.5(2) is not intended to reverse the order or judgment (nor the result
in the ﬁrst level of court). It would take extraordinary circumstances (such as an abandonment)
to see the court refuse entirely to enter the formal order or judgment. Especially as the deadline
for appealing since 2014 runs from pronouncement, not entry. The prime reason for the subrule
requiring leave is to let the court impose terms or conditions on late entry. Though the winner has
the prime obligation to draft the order or judgment, the other party can bring the matter back to
the court if the winner delays too long. So the losing party cannot argue not to enter the order or
judgment at all because of long delay and its prejudice.³
If time is extended, the order is enforced, and still speaks from the date of pronouncement.⁴
If an order is entered after the deadline (now 3 months) after it was granted contrary to R.9.5(2),
a judge should not later set it aside or declare it not to have been entered, without considering
the very lax law on extending time. Breach of that time limit does not produce a nullity. Extension
of the time should not be refused (even after the fact) unless it would cause real prejudice to the
other side. And if the party who now complains of the one-year delay was responsible for part of
it (e. g. by refusing to approve a reasonable draft of an order), that is all the more reason to extend
the time.⁵

1 September 7, 2022 Notice headed “Return to In-Person Chambers and Process for Orders and Filing”, found
in the “News & Announcements” part of the Court of King’s Bench website.
2 Cspregi v. Bygrove 2001 ABCA 108; Pro-Man Constr. v. T. -D. Bank (M) [1996] AJ #1214; Re Logan Est.
(#2) 2020 ABQB 308, JCE ES03 148137 (May 6) (¶’s 4-8). The decision is discretionary: Wright v. Murray
Rosen Pro. Corp.2005 ABCA 116. Orders were not entered within the 3 months set by Rr.9.5 and 14.77,
despite requests from the opposite party. The case management ofﬁcer properly extended the time to enter
them. Oversight in not telling the appellant about the request to the case management ofﬁcer to extend time
for entry, did no harm, and was excused: Alta. Health Serv. v. Wang 2018 ABCA 60, Calg 1701 0143 AC
(one JA Feb 13).

9-17
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.6
A formal entered order cannot be upset later on the grounds that it was signed by the Clerk
and not by a justice, where it had been granted by a justice, and the Rules at that time allowed
the Clerk to sign orders. (And if there had been delay in entry because the two parties could not
agree on the wording of the formal order, the Rules then provided that minutes of orders were to
be settled by the Clerk. ) So the Clerk’s doing so was valid. A practice to have justices settle the
minutes made no difference. An idea that a taxing ofﬁcer cannot award costs does not cut down
the rights of the Clerk or deputy Clerk to settle the minutes of an order and sign it and enter it,
even if the deputy Clerk is also taxing ofﬁcer.¹
On curing minor slips or omissions in the record, or
other exceptional circumstances, retroactively (“nunc pro tunc”), see the Sedgwick case.²
Effective Date of Judgments and Orders
9.6 Every judgment and every order, whether or not it has been entered, comes
into effect on
(a) the date of pronouncement, or
(b) if the Court orders the judgment or order to come into effect before
or after the date of pronouncement, the date so ordered.³

Related Provisions
9.1(2) (date of judgment) ; 9.9 (judgment for continuing damages) ; 14.78 (entry of judgment

after appeal)
Determining when a judgment takes effect can seem confusing, because there are some
overlapping Rules of Court, plus some common-law rules. However, the basic principle is simple.
A judgment is presumed to take effect at once, as soon as it is pronounced (or maybe even the
beginning of the day of pronouncement). It may take days or weeks thereafter to get a formal written
judgment entered, but the judgment is effective in the meantime.⁴
For special circumstances,
the court may expressly order otherwise and so make a judgment effective before, or after, its
pronouncement.⁵
Even if the judgment is technically only effective later, in the meantime it is
contempt of court now to interfere with future obedience to the order.⁶
“Pronouncement” refers to the time when the parties learn of the decision. If the judgment
is announced orally in open court, it is pronounced then. If judgment is reserved and written
reasons are later ﬁled, it is pronounced when the parties have a reasonable chance to get a copy
of the reasons.⁷
In Alberta, most times to appeal used to run from formal entry and service, not

R.9.6 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
pronouncement. But federal legislation often makes appeal times run from pronouncement. And
now Alberta’s 2014 civil appeal Rules (Part 14) make time to appeal run from pronouncement.¹
As an order or judgment is effective on pronouncement, before or without entry,²
time elapsed
thereafter does not count under limitations legislation.³
Courts have a power to backdate an order, and get appellate deference for that.⁴
Such an order
can be called a nunc pro tunc order. The power to antedate the effective date of a judgment can
be exercised at any time before formal entry of the judgment.⁵
But if the order does not provide
otherwise, it speaks from pronouncement, and cannot give a receiver the right to rents before
the order.⁶
If the Clerk loses an approved order and does not enter it, its entry or their effects (on
appeal) may be backdated (e. g. to avoid a new non-retroactive restriction on appeals).⁷
It is traditional to backdate the judgment to trial, if the plaintiff dies after trial but before judgment
is pronounced, so the court’s delay in deciding will not prejudice the plaintiff.⁸
A party who fails to enter an order or judgment in its favour and acts inconsistently with it, may
be taken to have abandoned the order or judgment.⁹
But an order to pay money into court and so
discharge a lien may be mandatory, so that the party getting the order cannot abandon it or avoid
it by not entering it.¹⁰
The Court of Appeal’s judgment rendered bad some proceedings taken without a necessary
step. Then the losing party, without alleging any error in the judgment, asked for a delay of many
months in its effect in other cases as a precedent. The Court of Appeal refused to do so, doubting
that it had power to postpone precedents, whether under Rule 9.6 or otherwise.¹¹
If a declaration is granted, but to have it take effect immediately would cause chaos or serious
problems with resources, or lead to bad decisions or re-litigation, the court can have it only come
into force months later. Even if there is no constitutional issue. The Judicature Act s.8 allows
remedies absolutely or on reasonable terms and conditions, and R.1.4 allows the Court to stay
the effect of a judgment or order.¹²
If an order is needed for a type of service and if the order is set aside later, that may retroactively
undo the service, even after the deadline for service has expired; but the Court may now extend
the time for service.¹³
[Footnote 7 continued from prior page]
decision nor pronouncement. The other side got any notice of decision only when it got a copy of the ﬁled
order; that is when the time to appeal started: Johnson v. Jordan 2013 ABCA 55, [2013] AR Uned 23 (one
JA Feb 12).

9-19
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.7(1)
Certiﬁed Copies
9.7(1) On entry of a judgment or order, the court clerk must, without additional
charge, certify a copy of it for the party who enters the judgment or order.¹
(2) A certiﬁed copy of a judgment or order has the same effect as the original.

Related Provisions
13.30 (certified copies of documents) ; 13.44 (duties of clerk)

A new legislated mode of proof is presumed not to be exclusive. The common law lets one
prove an order by its original or a sealed copy, and the order can be signed by Clerk.²
A previous criminal conviction is admissible as some evidence of any element of it now
contested, whether the previous conviction came from a trial or a guilty plea, and by a trial judge
or a jury. Convictions are presumed to be reliable, absent evidence to the contrary (or a successful
appeal from them. ) But (like all similar fact evidence) they are subject to exclusion if their prejudicial
effect outweighs their probative value.³
Anyone can buy a certiﬁed copy of a judgment; no standing is needed.⁴
Service of Judgments and Orders
9.8 Unless these rules otherwise provide or the Court otherwise orders, the party
who enters the judgment or order must serve a copy of the entered judgment or
order on each of the other parties.

Related Provisions
11.18 (service) ; 13.45 (giving clerk notice of order directing clerk to act).

If a tribunal sends out its decision to a party by courier, and receipt is acknowledged, that is
good service starting the appeal time running.⁵
DIVISION 2
DETERMINATION OF DAMAGES, JUDGMENT IN COUNTERCLAIMS
AND JUDGMENT AGAINST BENEFICIARIES
Determining Damages
9.9 The Court must determine damages for a continuing claim to the time the
Court makes its determination of the amount.⁶

9-20
R.9.9 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS

Related Provisions
9.6 (effective date of judgment)

Because of the Rule, damages for a continuing cause of action can be recovered up to trial,
and are not cut off at the date of the statement of claim. That includes quantum meruit for
services rendered at the defendant’s request.¹
At common law, a court could award damages only at trial, as a single lump sum which covers
past losses and makes allowance for estimated future losses. Now a court can order periodic
payments and review them later.²
Rule 9.9 can also be used to assess condominium fees accruing against a unit holder up to
judgment, or even later?³
Simple interest ordinarily runs from the pronouncement of the judgment,⁴
unless the judgment
sets different dates for payment. For instance, it runs from the date that each instalment under
a judgment awarding monthly payments should have been paid.⁵
Post-judgment interest begins
to run from the date of the judge’s reasons awarding the judgment or damages underlying, and
interest on costs begins on the same day, even interest on double costs because of an offer;
interest runs on costs before they are assessed.⁶
If the contractual rate of interest is higher than the statutory post-judgment rate, the creditor
cannot evade the latter by suing for a declaration that the amount is due.⁷
Does legislation allow
the court to raise the rate of post-judgment interest beyond that set by the legislation?⁸
Where a plaintiff is awarded and collects damages, but then on appeal the amount of damages
is reduced or liability removed, so that the money has to be repaid, for purposes of interest that
should be considered money payable under a judgment debt with interest running from the date
of the trial judgment.⁹
Assessing down a lawyer’s account already paid implies a duty to refund
the excess to the client, and so carries simple interest at the post-judgment rate from the date of
the assessment ofﬁcer’s allocatur.¹⁰

9-21
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.10
Judgment for Balance on Counterclaim
9.10 The Court may give judgment for the balance of money to be paid by one
party to the other when determining a counterclaim.¹

Related Provisions
3.56 (counterclaims) ; 3.59 (set-off)

What kind of judgment should the court give where there is a counterclaim? That is a complicated
question, and a number of Rules deal with it. Where there is no summary judgment, but a trial,
there are authorities for various possibilities.²
The impact of R.3.58 stating that a counterclaim
is an independent action, is unknown. The main question is whether to give judgment for each
party on each claim, or whether to net the two amounts and give a judgment for the balance only.³
Where there are two cross claims tried together at the same time, the trial judge can give a net
judgment in favour of one party, even if the technical Rules for set-off are not met.⁴
If a case is made out for summary judgment on one of the claims (i. e. , there is no arguable
defence to it), but the claim in the other direction is arguable, what judgment to give is less clear.⁵
Often judgment is given in one direction on summary judgment, but enforcement is stayed until
the other claim can be tried.⁶
The costs of the suit may be based on the net result, but the recipient of that may be considered
the winner; such a deduction need not be a mixed result for costs purposes.⁷
Judgment Against Beneﬁciaries
9.11 A personal representative or trustee entitled to a judgment or order for the
administration of an estate or the execution of a trust may have the judgment
or order made against any person beneﬁcially interested in the estate or trust.⁸
Information Note
If a party dies before judgment but after all evidence is heard, rule 4.35 [Death has no effect
on an action after evidence heard] applies.

R.9.12 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Related Provisions
2.11 (litigation representatives)

Though only the personal representative of an estate (executor or administrator) has the
authority to act with respect to the estate, he or she can delegate authority to others.¹
For limitations purposes, a “personal representative” is appointed or created only when a court
order or letters probate or letters of administration is issued by the court. Mere existence of a will
appointing an executor (plus death of the testator) is not enough.²

9.12 On application, the Court may correct a mistake or error in a judgment or

9-23
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.12
In general, a ﬁnal judgment cannot be changed once it is entered. Absent special legislation,
a King’s Bench judge has no power to vary a signed and entered judgment. That is the general
rule. That does not extend to a type of relief not addressed by the reasons for decision.¹
But R.
9.12 creates an important exception to that general rule. The interpretations of this Rule and the
common-law general rule against amending ﬁnal judgments, have been very contentious.
Of course it is axiomatic that (whether or not it has power to amend a past unappealed order),
the court which gave it should not amend if there is no good reason.²
The error acted on must be
plain and manifest.³
An order for summary judgment in a lawsuit but not yet ﬁnding the amount to be paid, is not
a ﬁnal judgment, and is not ﬁnally decisive of a case or suit, nor a ﬁnal decision of the whole
controversy. It is ﬁnal in the sense that it is binding, but as to one question. It does not end the suit,
and so R.4.33 still applies to the lawsuit. There is still a live action, until there is a ﬁnal monetary
judgment.⁴
2. Correcting Errors
The biggest mystery was whether after formal entry the court has a general power to rehear
and reconsider judgments or orders which purport to decide something ﬁnally, if there is no fraud.
The cases were divided on this point.⁵
They are more divided than they look, for most cases say
that only slips or true errors can be corrected,⁶
yet many cases deﬁne those categories very
broadly.⁷
One should look at binding authority, and not be distracted by a host of persuasive
authority.
It seems clear that an entered order or judgment may be corrected under R.9.12 when,
by a slip, it does not express what the court plainly intended to order,⁸
e. g. , if the reasons for
judgment award special damages of $420.00, but the formal judgment gives special damages of
$4, 200.00. An appellate judgment was amended to conform to the reasons, even after its entry
and certiﬁcation to the court below.⁹
The Supreme Court of Canada granted a motion to amend
the formal judgment of the court below, semble after the minutes of judgment had been settled.¹⁰

9-24
R.9.12 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
But the correction power under 2010 Rr.9.12 and 9.14 is now broader than under the previous
Rules.¹
The current test is reviewed.²
There is no point to the expense and delay of an appeal to
the Court of Appeal with an obvious conclusion, especially where the judge has created a public
error of law, where the King’s Bench judge concludes that he or she has made a clear error of law
impacting decisively on the result.³
This Rule applies only to slips in recording the court’s intention, and is not for re-arguing or re-
deciding past decisions. And the error must be based on facts existing at the time of the decision,
not on facts which have occurred or changed since.⁴
It is possible that R.12.18 may also apply, and R.1.5 probably does also; they refer to an
error in the formal judgment or order, not to an error in the reasons for judgment or pronounced
oral judgment which were then faithfully transcribed into the formal judgment.⁵
So if an afﬁdavit
or an incomplete review of the court ﬁle gives a wrong amount, which then goes into the
entered judgment, it cannot be corrected.⁶
Once declarations (as to a lease) are given under
an originating notice and the judgment entered, the judge is functus and cannot amend the
judgment, especially not to add a party.⁷
Rule 9.12 or its equivalent is about correcting the formal
judgment, not correcting errors in the reasons for judgment or implications from them.⁸
A judge
cannot “correct” an entered judgment because of alleged lack of evidence to support one item.⁹
Especially if the application is to one judge, but the reasons are by several judges.¹⁰
Still less can
the court reopen an entered judgment, even to hear new arguments not made before.¹¹
3. Procedure
Usually R.9.12 requires an application by one party to the suit, but the court itself can correct
a plain error in expressing in an entered order or judgment in expressing what the court plainly
intended.¹²
A request to change the court’s reasons because they are in error (and the formal judgment
is not yet entered) must be made under R.9.13, not R.9.12.¹³
What corrections or additions to a
transcript of earlier oral reasons for decision are proper, under the Court of King’s Bench usual
practice, are listed.¹⁴
If the judge thinks that additional reasons or content are needed, or actual
changes to what was said are needed, then supplementary reasons should be signed and ﬁled.¹⁵

9-25
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.12
The procedure is to see if there was error, then decide whether the court should use its power
and correct the error.¹
Fact ﬁndings on evidence should not be changed later.²
Nor should the correction application
be made into issues not explored before the decision was initially given.³
4. Interlocutory Orders
See also R.9.15(4)
The Court of Appeal discusses when an interlocutory order can later be set aside or varied,
even if it is by consent. Previous decisions (under the 1968 Rules) are not quite on point. Consent
orders differ, depending whether they are merely procedural or whether they settle the lawsuit.
Consent may refer to a real contract, or just no objection. If the order is just interlocutory and
does not settle the substantive dispute, there is no contract, and discretion governs whether later
to alter it. Looking at the narrow grounds to upset a contract is wrong if there is no contract or
settlement of the substantive dispute. And R.9.15(4) is clearly discretionary anyway. Rules 1.5
and 13.5 support this approach also.⁴
Interlocutory rulings by a trial judge can be revised by her if circumstances change.⁵
C. Omissions
On matters not fully dealt with (such as costs), see R.9.14 and notes.
If the judge’s written reasons clearly deal with a question but the formal judgment does not, it
is better to amend the formal order to insert it, than to ﬁle a second formal order.⁶
Judgments for fraud or dishonesty must recite that fraud etc. to survive bankruptcy, but that
gives problems if the bankruptcy is later or the dishonesty is hidden until later, especially where
creditors had been trying to get the relevant information, so sometimes the judgment can later be
corrected.⁷
D. Improper Entry
An entered order or judgment can probably be corrected if improperly entered, e. g. , without
notice to or approval of form by, the opposing lawyer.⁸
Especially if it is understood there is to be
a motion to rehear, or there was an escrow or promise barring entry.⁹
A purported Provincial Court civil judgment was ﬁled in the Court of King’s Bench for
enforcement, but it was later found to be a forgery, there being no such judgment or even lawsuit,
and the docket number not being valid. There was no such Clerk or court ﬁling stamp for Provincial

R.9.13 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Court. So the appeal court Justice of King’s Bench ordered that the new King’s Bench ﬁle be
immediately vacated nunc pro tunc, and a formal order doing that was to be prepared at once,
to be available if there was any attempt to enforce the non-existent judgment.¹
E. Appeal
Decisions under R.9.12 on whether to amend a judgment better to express the court’s intent
(or not) are peculiarly within the province of the court making them, and should not be upset on
appeal.²
F. When Order Takes Effect
See also R.9.6.
Whether or not there is a power to correct errors, an erroneous order has effect at once, and
an order or judgment of a superior court is never a nullity. Unless and until an order or judgment is
corrected, it has full force as written.³
It must be obeyed, and may be enforced, until it is revoked
or corrected.⁴
It cannot be collaterally attacked in another proceeding⁵
even during a contempt
proceeding to enforce it. Even if it is alleged to be based on an unconstitutional law.⁶
Unless
maybe its face shows lack of jurisdiction.⁷
G. Delay
Where the ﬂaw was in the appeal court’s previous formal judgment, but the appellants did not
object promptly, and instead litigated the judgment’s effect in the court below, the appellant got no
relief against the costs in the court below, though the appeal court varied the judgment.⁸
Re-opening Case
9.13 At any time before a judgment or order is entered, the Court may
(a) vary the judgment or order, or
(b) on application, and if the Court is satisﬁed there is good reason to
do so, hear more evidence and change or modify its judgment or
order or reasons for it.

Related Provisions
8.20 (non-suite) ; 8.24 (failure to prove a fact at trial) ; 9.16 (by whom applications are to be

decided)
A. General
The formatting of the Rule looks odd; should there be a line break and end of indentation, after
“application” in paragraph (b) ?

9-27
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.13
Rule 9.13 is broader than its predecessor R.339, and whether it applies depends on the
factual circumstances.¹
This is the Rule to use to correct the reasons which the court has given, not R.9.12.²
Rule 9.13
does not affect a decision by a judge which is not a change of his previous decision, but merely
deciding a small procedural point which had been overlooked the previous day.³
There seem to be no exceptions to the court’s power to vary or revoke a judgment or order
before it is formally entered (signed and ﬁled). Rule 9.13 is not conﬁned to the narrow time period
between pronouncing a verdict or reasons for decision, and entry of formal judgment; indeed it
can be used before pronouncing a verdict.⁴
Until then, the judge has jurisdiction to decide that it
was wrong, and pronounce one to the opposite effect.⁵
But whether to use that power is a different question; the judge should not do so lightly.⁶
This
power is exceptional, and to be used rarely.⁷
The threshold to reopen the matter under R.9.13
is high; a high likelihood must be shown.⁸
That is necessary to avoid abusive case splitting, and
promote ﬁnality in litigation. A party must put its best foot forward the ﬁrst time.⁹
Six criteria for such
reopening are listed.¹⁰
The power under 2010 R.9.13 is broader than the power in the previous R.
339, but whether to exercise the power is now narrower. And whether to do so depends in part
on whether new evidence is properly admitted.

9-28
R.9.13 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
If the trial court has made a blatant error, it should try to ﬁx it and not force the parties to bring
an unnecessary appeal. But the putative error must be plain and manifest; this is not for a second
kick at the can. Even though the judge is asked to rule on whether he or she made a mistake
earlier.¹
A new argument (or one withheld before²
) is not enough ground to amend a judgment.³
The
Rule is suitable for very probable objective errors, but not for revisiting judgment calls.⁴
It is not
usually a proper avenue for a party to submit newly-minted arguments not thought of before, to
shore up gaps in evidence, to reconsider a litigation strategy, to split the case, or to get a second
kick at the can. The tests are inﬂuenced by the Palmer case for new evidence on appeal, the
“practically conclusive” item being their restated as expected to change the result when taken with
all the existing evidence.⁵
Though a judge can reconsider his or her order before entry, it should
occur only in limited circumstances: clear error or important new facts; this is not the time for
reargument.⁶
A new contrary precedent is not enough if it is merely persuasive, given the limited
scope of R.9.13.⁷
For example, a damages award should be ﬁnal, irrespective of what the plaintiff
later spends the money on.⁸
There is a review of older cases on what are not good substantive
grounds to reopen a court’s decision and the authorities on that.⁹
The Supreme Court of Canada can vary its pronounced judgment, before formal judgment is
entered.¹⁰

9-29
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.13
On a motion to rehear, interest was revised to run from the trial judgment (not the appellate
one),¹
but no costs of the rehearing were given because of failure to ask for that before.²
B. New Evidence
One who loses a trial which he defends on one set of facts cannot (absent fraud) then sue to
set aside the judgment because of newly-discovered evidence which establishes an inconsistent
set of facts.³
Any newly-discovered evidence must not have been previously discoverable by due
diligence. The courts apply this limit very strictly.⁴
And any delay must be explained properly.⁵
Delay in putting forward new evidence to correct an order gives the judge a choice whether to
correct it.⁶
The new evidence must be practically conclusive. It must entirely change the entire case, not
just some ingredient or aspect of the case, and not merely be an additional ingredient.⁷
New events since judgment may not be a ground to reopen judgment if the applicant helped
create them, and she did not have clean hands.⁸
A court may reopen a proceeding if it had been
misled as to the record or the issues, if there is a risk of miscarriage of justice, or good evidence
of ineffective counsel.⁹
When another issue became evident after pronouncement and before entry of formal judgment,
more evidence could be put to the trial judge then, especially when he told counsel that he was
prepared to consider the issue. It was too late to put in the evidence on appeal.¹⁰
Is family law litigation laxer and more permissive because it is prospective?¹¹
But the
Saskatchewan Court of Appeal disagrees and says after some time, most matrimonial suits see
some new smaller changes in the facts, and so there should be no appeal and no reopening, and
for bit changes one should use a Rule like this one, not appeal.¹²

9-30
R.9.13 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
C. Procedure
Rule 9.13 sets up no particular procedure.¹
A request to reopen and re-decide is not a two-stage hearing, and material change need not
be shown to get a full hearing (though showing a material change may put a practical onus on
the respondent).²
Rule 9.13 was used where the respondent’s solicitor had notice of the motion, but sent an
agent who had no instructions to do anything.³
On changing costs, see Rr.10.30 and 9.14 and commentary.
On orders containing a clause allowing later applications to vary, see Trimay Wear Plate.⁴
A
judge could vary another judge’s previous order (even if the earlier one was by consent), because
the matter was adjourned by the previous judge to a named date, and it was expressly interim and
to last until “further order of the court”.⁵
Use of this Rule ends with entry of the order. “Entry” means ﬁling a formal judgment, not ﬁling
written reasons.⁶
Normally, no one could alter the costs award in an entered formal Court of Appeal judgment,
even if it turned out that (unknown to the panel) a formal offer had been bettered. But it could be
reconsidered in the very unusual circumstances of the request of both parties, and where some
error in wording the order of one justice of appeal in chambers seemed likely, leading to conﬂicting
orders.⁷
And where the Court of Appeal decided an appeal in the belief that key reasons of the
trial court were never recorded, but in fact they were, after judgment the Court of Appeal ordered
the appeal reargued.⁸
The court reheard argument on the proper date to convert damages to Canadian currency.⁹
An order denying leave to appeal (by one justice of appeal) is ﬁnal and not open to
reconsideration much later.¹⁰
An unsuccessful attempt to reargue an appeal should lead to solicitor-client costs?¹¹
Of course the court can later recognize that an order has expired because of its own terms or
conditions, e. g. if the proceeding pending which an interlocutory injunction was given has been
discontinued.¹²
A fairly common ploy of not coming to an application of which one had timely notice and then
moving to set the order aside, must fail, even if the non-attending party then made himself or
herself unable to attend.¹³

2 Unterschultz v. Clark 2021 ABQB 492, JCE 4803 17126 (Jun 25) (¶’s 31-42).

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.14
Rule 3.65 on amending pleadings is free standing, and Pt.9 on reopening proceedings need
not be complied with, especially where the merits of the claims have never been adjudicated.¹
Rule 9.13 is not for a stay pending appeal, still less a stay of the precedential affect of the
judgment being appealed.²
Further or Other Order After Judgment or Order Entered
9.14 On application, the Court may, after a judgment or order has been entered,
make any further or other order that is required, if
(a) doing so does not require the original judgment or order to be varied,
and
(b) the further or other order is needed to provide a remedy to which a
party is entitled in connection with the judgment or order.
Information Note
If an application is made under this rule, note that rule 6.3(1) [Applications generally] provides
for an application to be made after judgment.

Related Provisions
6.3(1) (applications after judgment) ; 9.25 (order granting possession of land) ; 10.28 (costs

awards after trial); 9.16 (by whom applications are to be decided) ; 13.1 (any judge can act) ;
14.38(2) (d) (further directions after appeal)
A. Generally
Rule 9.14 should be better known. Rule 9.14 is broader than the previous analogous Rule.³
The general rule is that one cannot vary a judgment once entered.⁴
The court is sometimes said
then to be functus officio.⁵
Rule 9.14 provides a narrow exception where one is not really varying
the judgment, but just supplementing it or working it out,⁶
or the court decides the effect of its
earlier judgment.⁷
Rule 9.14 does not stop a judge from clarifying, supplementing or working out
the terms of her previous order, such as a grant of habeas corpus.⁸
Under R.9.14, the judge
can later clarify whether the interim support which he or she ordered ratiﬁes similar voluntary
payments, or is in addition.⁹

R.9.14 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
But R.9.14 does not enable the court to vary the previous order.¹
If new evidence is tendered to support the request to add to the judgment, the 4-part Palmer
test for new evidence must be met.²
But Rule 9.14 cannot be used to change an error in an entered ﬁnal order.³
By its express
terms, Rule 9.14 cannot support an application to reconsider and vary a judgment previously
pronounced.⁴
Absent special legislation, a King’s Bench judge has no power to vary a signed and
entered judgment (here to order additional security). That is the general rule.1968 R.330 allowed
ensuring relief, but it had to be relief which the original judgment gave. That does not extend to a
type of relief not addressed by the reasons for decision.⁵

The following are cases where further direction or ampliﬁcation of remedies in a previous
order were given: apportioning among two named topics the earlier costs award;⁶
clariﬁcation
of an order in order to satisfy Revenue Canada;⁷
amending costs directions (even after entry of
judgment, given R.10.30);⁸
more evidence about costs;⁹
awarding trial costs after the substantive
judgment is afﬁrmed on appeal;¹⁰
or doing so after the substantive result is varied on appeal;¹¹
working out certain amounts under an order nisi of foreclosure (but not others) ;¹²
clarifying an
order about possible future leave to appeal if sale proceeds were not enough;¹³
stating that the

aiding enforcement of a
matrimonial property equalization order, aiding enforcement, even lifting a bankruptcy stay.²
One cannot use R.9.14 to rule on an aspect of a settlement and consent judgment which was
not expressly dealt with.³
A superior court judge can maintain supervision over the enforcement and obedience to his
or her order, particularly under the Charter, and no legislation should be read as limiting that, and
where the Charter is concerned it could not limit it.⁴
After a matrimonial property distribution or
equalization order, the trial judge can give orders to aid enforcement, even to lift the stay created
by the “debtor” spouse’s later bankruptcy, if other creditors would not be prejudiced.
Rule 9.14 does not allow a declaration after entry of judgment that the tort is also a crime so
there is no exemption from execution.⁵
Even on a costs issue after trial, an afﬁdavit cannot contain evidence on the merits of the suit
varying from the trial evidence.⁶
Similarly for tax withholdings from a judgment.⁷
Setting Aside, Varying and Discharging Judgments and Orders
9.15(1) On application, the Court may set aside, vary or discharge a judgment or
an order, whether ﬁnal or interlocutory, that was made
(a) without notice to one or more affected persons, or
(b) following a trial or hearing at which an affected person did not appear
because of an accident or mistake or because of insufﬁcient notice
of the trial or hearing.⁸
(2) Unless the Court otherwise orders, the application must be made within 20
days after the earlier of
(a) the service of the judgment or order on the applicant, and
(b) the date the judgment or order ﬁrst came to the applicant’s attention.⁹
(3) The Court may, on any terms the Court considers just,
(a) permit a defence to be ﬁled by a party who has been noted in default,
(b) set aside, vary or discharge a judgment granted upon application
against a defendant who was noted in default, or whose statement
of defence was struck out under rule 3.37, or

A. Introduction
On the difference between judgments and orders, see R.9.1n. On setting aside consent
orders, see R.3.35 n.
A previous decision of a court cannot be ignored or upset unless the object of this present
proceeding is simply to reverse, vary or nullify the earlier order or judgment. Even in ex parte or
fraud cases, that must be done directly by an application to set aside, or an appeal, not collaterally,
which means not in a proceeding where the attack is only incidental to something else.²
Counsel has a duty to sign the formal order (as to form) even if in the meantime he has been
ﬁred by his client.³
Where a formal judgment is approved and entered with both parties knowing that the losing
party is about to move to have the matter (an appeal) reheard, indeed the judgment is ﬁled after
the written application to rehear is, the court is not functus, especially where the winning party
did not intend to bar the motion. The court has inherent power to act where the formal judgment
was entered in the face of an obstacle such as a Rule barring entry, an escrow, a promise not to
enter, or a motion to reopen where no one intends entry to bar that motion.⁴
It is also necessary
that there be no delay or beneﬁt taken from the judgment, or prejudice to any party, and that
justice favours reopening.⁵
And details should not be added by ex parte application, to an order
got at an opposed hearing.⁶
Where the ﬂaw was in the previous formal judgment on appeal, but the appellants did not
object promptly, and instead litigated the judgment’s effect in the court below, the appellant got no
relief against the costs in the court below, though the appellate court varied the formal judgment.⁷

r.15.

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.15(4)
This Rule is no help where a ﬁnal entered order in a foreclosure comes from a positive error
at an earlier stage.¹
Changes to ex parte or default orders should be sought ﬁrst from the King’s Bench under R.
9.15, and not go directly to the Court of Appeal.²
B. Setting Aside Ex Parte Orders
1. Introduction
The 2010 Rules often eschew traditional Canadian legal terminology.³
“Ex parte” means heard
without notice to another party (or to any other party).⁴
There is a general discussion of how to have ex parte orders reviewed, set aside, or modiﬁed,
and when to apply before a judge, and when to appeal to the Court of Appeal.⁵
If a party did not get notice or a chance to oppose a proposed order, it is better for him or
her to move in King’s Bench under this Rule; an appeal is too expensive and likely too slow.⁶
The
functus officio doctrine does not bar re-opening an order if it was given without notice (ex parte),
or with old insufﬁcient evidence, whether or not the justice or Applications Judge knew the notice
was insufﬁcient or lacking.⁷
Where an argument was not made to an Applications Judge who was hearing an ex parte
motion, someone dissatisﬁed with the order should move to set it aside, not appeal to a justice.⁸
The Court of Appeal ordinarily does not hear an appeal from an ex parte order, and the better
relief is to go to the trial court and move to set it aside, particularly in a child custody or access
matter.⁹
It is doubtful that the tests for opening up default judgment (n. C below) apply to an application
under R.9.15(1). Explanation for the non-appearance would be needed.¹⁰
Succeeding on an ex parte application is no assurance, representation, or advice by the
court, and ex parte orders are always subject to recall with more evidence or argument. And it is
the applicant’s own choice to give no notice when applying.¹¹
If a respondent got a copy of the written Application and appeared in chambers, there is
probably no general power to set aside the resulting order, whether for non-disclosure or
otherwise.¹²
Where there was proper service, or notice, and failure to show up, the remedy was in
R.9.15(1) (b).¹³

9-36
R.9.15(4) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
2. Scope of This Rule
The rules for setting aside ex parte orders were applied to a case where counsel for the
respondent had notice of the motion and when it would be heard, but could not attend (because
of another court engagement elsewhere) and explained that inability from the outset, and did not
attend the hearing in question.¹
Any party who did not get notice of an application can use this Rule; the order made was ex
parte so far as he or she is concerned. The same is true of a “consent” order which he or she did
not consent to, or which went beyond what was consented to,²
or one where a party without status
consented.³
Or where the notice was very defective.⁴
An ex parte ﬁat can be set aside just as a full order could.⁵
One judge can set aside another
judge’s ex parte order.⁶
If the original order was given in busy regular morning chambers, that
judge would probably not remember it, so there is little point to selecting the ﬁrst judge to hear the
application to set it aside.⁷
But now see R.9.16.
Someone served abroad with an Alberta statement of claim can move to set the ex parte
order for service ex juris. aside under R.11.31 (thus avoiding defending or attorning to Alberta’s
jurisdiction), and need not move under R.9.15.⁸
An Applications Judge cannot open up a judgment where it was given by a justice on notice,
even if the defendant did not show up by accident.⁹
3. Procedure
An ex parte order may not be set aside unless notice of the application to set aside is given
to all interested parties.¹⁰
The motion to set aside is heard de novo, so the mere fact of the ex parte order ordinarily
should not change the onus of proof.¹¹
A motion to set aside an ex parte order is heard de novo,

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.15(4)
and the party who got the order still has the onus of proof of the merits.¹
And so new evidence
to support the original order can in the judge’s discretion be admitted then.²
Of course the party
who got no notice before can now adduce evidence and cross-examine on the original evidence.³
On a motion to set aside an ex parte order, the court has discretion to admit fresh evidence.⁴
The discretion must be exercised properly, without exclusion of evidence for delay where another
remedy would sufﬁce, and without any error in the Rules or principles weighed or not weighed,
and in context of the history of the application, seeing what realistic chances that party had earlier
to know of, get, see the need for or relevance of, or to proffer, that evidence earlier.⁵
Delay in moving to set aside can be reason to let the original order stand.⁶
4. Non-Disclosure
If the person getting an ex parte order did not disclose to the original justice or Applications
Judge facts which he or she should have known had some materiality to the motion, special
results ﬂow.⁷
Full and fair disclosure is critically important on ex parte applications.⁸
There is a
duty to disclose such facts,⁹
especially as the judges cannot read the ﬁles in morning chambers,
and so have to rely on what counsel tell them.¹⁰
That onus of disclosure in getting an ex parte
order is especially heavy when an Anton Piller order is sought.¹¹
Such nondisclosure puts a heavy
onus on the party trying to uphold the order, which may later be set aside on that ground alone.¹²
Even worse, on an ex parte application for an order, misstating some facts, or making factual
assertions beyond what the evidence ﬁled would reasonably support, is serious, and was one
of the important reasons for upsetting the two orders thus obtained.¹³

R.9.15(4) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
fraud in order to set aside the order.¹
Usually the existence of counsel on the other side must be
disclosed.²
But the order need not be set aside if the nondisclosure was innocent and did little harm.³
Misstatements of immaterial facts, or slight errors in material facts, have no effect.⁴
Conversely, if
the omissions do harm, the resulting order is not saved by the fact that the fault was the judge’s
for declining an offer to go through the background, especially if the judge has not had time to
read the papers.⁵
Such nondisclosure can even be a ground for damages, in a strong case,⁶
and certainly for
costs or reduced fees,⁷
or costs payable personally by the offending counsel.⁸
If one party gets an ex parte injunction and then an added party gets a similar injunction on
notice in the same suit, that second injunction is not ex parte, and the added party had neither
duties of full disclosure, nor limits on adding more evidence on the motion to conﬁrm or upset the
original injunction.⁹
The judge should not bar in advance further evidence without seeing what it
is.¹⁰
5. Miscellaneous
On a motion to set aside a previous ex parte order for non-disclosure, now that the court
has more evidence from the defendant, the onus still rests on the plaintiff to continue the order
(injunction).¹¹
If on a further hearing on notice, the grounds for such an order seem to lack evidence,
the order may be set aside.¹²
It is arguable that a hearing to conﬁrm or upset an ex parte order, being de novo, is heard
under R.6.3(1), not R.6.6(2).¹³

9-39
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.15(4)
Rule 9.15 now allows “affected persons” and hence non-parties, to move to vary a judgment
or order, but only in limited circumstances. The phrase “affected persons” means those legally
affected by the order or decision, not those with a personal or moral stake. There is no authority
for allowing them to attack a judgment because of their reputation, and the whole Rule is about
becoming unfairly bound by a court order by not being heard, without any fault of one’s own.¹
Even if an ex parte order were later set aside, it does not follow that evidence gathered
pursuant to it would be excluded, or that proceedings would be stayed.²
Costs of an ex parte interlocutory application can cause problems.³
Where there is an ex
parte application and then a motion to set the order aside, for costs purposes they should be
treated together as one.⁴
When an order is set aside, often costs “thrown away” are awarded.
Those do not always have to be partial indemnity, and sometimes can be full indemnity.⁵
Rule 9.15’s 20-day time limit to change or set aside an order requires irreparable injury to bar
extending the time, so mere delay does not bar amending or setting aside an ex parte order. A
year’s delay in moving plus more months’ delay in seeking to extend the time, were also relevant
factors.⁶
Can an ex parte order later be retroactively declared a nullity, by another judge?⁷
That
suggestion seems hard to reconcile with basic principles.⁸
A statutory application to annul an
order is a better remedy (preserving rights of innocent non-parties) than an order to rescind the
ﬁrst order, even if there was insufﬁcient notice before the ﬁrst order.⁹
An insurer has sufﬁcient interest to have standing to argue that a life insurance policy is
exempt, and to set aside an ex parte order dealing with it.¹⁰
It was proper to rescind a judgment got through breach of an undertaking to ﬁle proof of
service, which was impossible to perform because there had not been service! So the chambers
judge could unwind that and all later ancillary steps.¹¹
C. Opening Up Default Judgment
1. Default Signed or Noted by the Clerk of the Court
Subrule (2) on orders without notice does not apply to default judgments or noting in default,
because the statement of claim is notice.¹²
