R.9.15(4) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
After default judgment is signed, it is very common for the defendant then to ask the court to
“open up” the judgment, i. e. , to revoke it and let the defendant defend the lawsuit on the merits.¹
This happens in three different situations:²
Situation 1.
There is neither ﬂaw in the judgment nor in the plaintiff’s procedure leading to it, so the
defendant is seeking an indulgence.³
To get judgment opened up then, such a defendant
must show⁴
by sworn evidence all of 3 things:⁵
(a) he has an arguable ground of defence,⁶
by fairly speciﬁc evidence,⁷

R.9.15(4) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
In this situation #1, the court usually imposes terms and conditions on the defendant.
The defendant always has to pay the plaintiff’s costs thrown away,¹
and often also pay
the costs of the motion to open up.²
The principle is that to make the indulgence fair, all
prejudice to the plaintiff must be repaired.³
If execution (enforcement) has been levied,
it may be ordered to stand pending trial, and any fruits of execution (enforcement) may
have to remain in court pending trial.⁴
If the defendant’s position is somewhat dubious, he
may have to put up large security for costs.⁵
Or the court can impose terms on refusing to
open up a default judgment, e. g. , to refer one supposed defence to arbitration.⁶
Imposing
terms as a condition of opening up is discretionary and hard to upset on appeal.⁷
If there
would have to be an assessment (trial) of damages anyway, it is easier to get a noting in
default opened up.⁸
Situation 2.
There is some (more than trivial) ﬂaw in the default judgment or the procedure leading to
it. In that event, the defendant is entitled as of right to open up the default judgment if the

Al-Shiraida 2011 ABQB 54, [2011] AR Uned 87 (Feb 2) (¶’s 25-6) ; Givens v. Paulmac, supra; B. M. v.
Cochrane, supra; dicta in Syncrude Can. v. Highland Consulting Grp.2013 ABQB 594, 571 AR 338 (¶
32); Atl. (HS) Finl. v. Punjabi (M) 2017 ABQB 87, 52 Alta LR(6th) 90. On which see the C. P. E. , Chapter 17,
Part L. Rule 9.15 sets a 20-day time limit but expressly says that the court can extend it. In any event, it is
about judgments and orders and does not apply to a noting in default: Al-Ghamdi v. R.2017 ABQB 169, 98
CPC(7th) 146 (¶’s 86-91). The 20 days can be extended if this delay was counsel’s fault, and if the defendant
applicant earlier had told the plaintiff that she (the defendant) intended to apply to open up: Kraushar v.
Kraushar, supra (¶ 9). But delay before learning of the lawsuit (or default proceedings) does not count:
Rough v. Cold L. F. N.2016 ABQB 153, 94 CPC(7th) 226; Al-Ghamdi v. R. , supra (¶’s 86-91). Noting in
default was not set aside at trial as the defendant moving did not move promptly. She testiﬁed at trial, but
her opponents did not anticipate that, and were not prepared: B. M. v. Gomivnik 2005 ABQB 768, 389 AR
299. Or a promise by the defendant’s spouse or employer to “take care of it”: Van Lent v. Place 2013 ABQB
494, JCE 1103 17838 (Aug 30). Quaere. But cf. Royal Bank v. Goebel, infra. Arguable motives and delay
in moving helped bar opening up, in Wardley v. Wardley, supra. But long gaps have often been excused if
the defendant did not know of the suit during the gap.

R.9.15(4) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
right to open up.¹
The defendant does not normally need to prove more than the ﬂaw, and
usually the court cannot impose any terms and conditions.²
Note that the plaintiff can
unilaterally amend his or her pleadings, including dropping parties.³
If the defendant’s lawyer asks for time to defend before default is noted, and the plaintiff’s
lawyer unreasonably refuses and both show up at the Clerk’s counter simultaneously
(with a statement of defence and default papers respectively), any default then entered
may be unconditionally opened up.⁴
It was error for a King’s Bench judge to give default judgment against a defendant who
had given counsel for the plaintiff a draft of his statement of defence, had tried to ﬁle it
with the Clerk, had had it bounced, told plaintiff’s counsel all that, and had tried to retain
counsel. Counsel for the plaintiff knew all that, yet when applying for the judgment, told
the judge none of that. Though the defendant applied to set aside the noting in default
(before the plaintiff moved for judgment), by some procedural irregularity the court heard
the judgment motion before the opening-up motion.⁵
Situation 3.
The procedure leading up to judgment was correct, but judgment was inadvertently
entered for too large an amount.⁶
There is no longer a speciﬁc Rule covering that situation,
but despite the omission, probably the court can amend the amount of the judgment, or
send the case to an assessment hearing and ensure that the defendant gets notice of it,⁷
but otherwise let the judgment stand. An afﬁdavit of inadvertence from the plaintiff might
be necessary to get this relief and not upset the whole judgment.
The remedy of opening up default judgment (in situation #1) is discretionary, and so subject to
considerations of fairness.⁸
Decisions under R.9.15(1) (b) are discretionary, and hard to upset on
appeal.⁹
A plaintiff should be able to rely on a judgment which it has obtained, and a lot of wasted
effort and legal fees (i. e. thrown-away costs) are relevant.¹⁰

9-45
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.15(4)
Usually a defendant moving to open up needs to tender a draft statement of defence, but not
if it can make out then a case for summary dismissal of the suit.¹
2. Procedure on Opening Up
Someone other than the defendant may apply to open up a default judgment.²
Where legislation
lets a “party affected” apply to set aside an order, that phrase can have a wide meaning, though
the context is very important.³
A plaintiff probably loses any right to open up its own default
judgment once it levies execution (enforcement) and collects under the execution.⁴
But until then
the plaintiff can so apply, e. g. because the defendant has gone bankrupt and the plaintiff wants
to plead fraud.⁵
If default judgment is given against two defendants who are not alternately liable, and one
gets it opened up, the plaintiff has not elected to take judgment against one, and the one who
opened up is not thereby freed of liability.⁶
Noting in default is not conclusive until judgment, and even a default judgment need not be
set aside if it does not decide the point now in question; so a foreclosure order nisi/sale may not
decide personal liability for the deﬁciency, especially if it has been decided to handle the in rem
and in personam aspects separately.⁷
Under situation #2 above (faulty judgment), the plaintiff will not get costs. Probably the
defendant will get costs, and if the ﬂaws and fault were actual misconduct by the plaintiff or its
lawyer, the costs may be very heavy.⁸
Where a big penalty was levied and then paid, but most of the ﬁndings of guilt were set
aside, the amounts should not be repaid before the Supreme Court of Canada dismissed a leave
application. All sums should be held separately and involve interest.⁹
The court was very critical of a plaintiff’s counsel who applied without notice for further sale
orders to execute on (enforce) a default judgment, though plaintiff knew that the defendant
had counsel trying to open up the default and did not tell the judge any of that. That positive
misrepresentation led to heavy personal costs against the plaintiff’s counsel.¹⁰
If a party noted in default is appealing the corollary relief parts of a divorce judgment, the most
effective thing is to move in King’s Bench to have the noting in default opened up before the Court
of Appeal hears the appeal.¹¹
If default judgment (or noting in default) is opened up, the plaintiff’s only remedy is to appeal;
he cannot sue the judge who opened up nor the opposing lawyers.¹²

9-46
R.9.15(4) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
3. Judgment by Justice or Applications Judge
Rule 9.15(3) does not apply to a judgment granted by a justice or Applications Judge, after a
motion in chambers or at trial. If that judgment was on notice, appeal is the only remedy. If it was
ex parte, R.9.15(1) (a) is the remedy. If it was by inadvertent failure to show up in chambers or
for trial, R.9.15(1) (b) is.¹
Once judgment is entered after the defendant did not appear at trial, the
judge is functus officio. Rule 9.15 does not apply. It is doubtful whether R.9.15(b) can be used to
open up, but if it can, only within the 20-day time limit set in it.²
Someone noted in default cannot
just move to open up a consequent order later made against him or her, such as an assessment
of damages. The defendant must ﬁrst move for and get opening up the noting in default, and
do so promptly, or show good reason for any delay.³
But if the later order was given in morning
chambers, likely the defendant need not apply to the same judge.⁴
D. Failure to Appear on an Application
Rule 9.15(1) (b) is often overlooked, and sometimes lawyers instead try to ﬁt such cases within
R.9.12.⁵
Those attempts are more understandable if what is in question is summary judgment,
not a mere order, for then it is not clear whether this Rule applies or not. A combination of Rr.1.5,
1.7(2) and 9.12 should offer relief in cases of a true slip or accident leading to a judgment.
On which judge can act under R.9.15, see R.9.16.
Note the 20-day time limit under this Rule; but if one had a good excuse for the delay, it could
be extended under R.12.5 (and the words of this Rule). Rule 9.15 cannot be used if the time limit
has passed and there is no application to extend it.⁶
And evidence would be needed to extend
that time limit.⁷
Rule 9.15(1) (b) does not apply if the application was full and proper and served in time, and
the respondent chose not to oppose the application,⁸
or gives no reason why he did not come to
the court.⁹
Then there may be no relief possible, except maybe an appeal.¹⁰
Can the absent party
later have the order set aside? Only if non-attendance was caused by an “accident or mistake”,
i. e. would not have happened without that certain event. A decision not to do something is not an

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.15(4)

Being out of town (even
a long way) and deciding not to come back, is no such accident. That is doubly so if the counsel
who won had not been told of that situation. Relief may be given under R.9.15(1) (b) if the failure
to appear was inadvertent.²
Rule 9.15(1) (b) was used where the Clerk put two related cross-
applications in two different courtrooms, so one lawyer went to the wrong courtroom.³
If there was
a problem with service of the application but the evidence of the respondent was dubious, he may
have to put up security to have summary judgment against him set aside.⁴
Failure to answer undertakings or to give one’s lawyer one’s current address or telephone
number may mean that a default order cannot be upset.⁵
Special care is needed in a child custody case where a parent does not show up for a
scheduled hearing; the judge should ask questions and try to ﬁnd the cause, though without
violating any privilege.⁶
Where one lawyer is counsel of record for several parties, and attends a hearing, then after
entry of the order, no party whom that lawyer represented can ask to have it reopened because
there are other arguments which were not made then. The court is functus officio.⁷

was ground to open up the resulting order.⁸
A judge made a (conﬁdentiality) order which said that

That permitted an application to set aside the order entirely. And no change of circumstances
seemed necessary.⁹
On the limited role of an Applications Judge, see the Hunik case.¹⁰
E. Interlocutory Orders
On when a party should be given an adjournment of an application, see commentary on R.
6.3, supra. On one party not showing up for an application which is then decided in his or her
absence, see Part D above.
The Court of Appeal discusses when an interlocutory order can later be set aside or varied,
even if it is by consent. Previous decisions (under the 1968 Rules) are not quite on point. Consent
orders differ, depending whether they are procedural or whether they settle the lawsuit. Consent
may refer to a real contract, or just no objection. If the order is just interlocutory and does not
settle the substantive dispute, there is no contract, and discretion governs whether later to alter

R.9.15(4) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
it. Looking at the narrow grounds to upset a contract is wrong if there is no contract or settlement
of the substantive dispute. And R.9.15(4) is clearly discretionary anyway. Rules 1.5 and 13.5
support this approach also.¹
WARNING: It is important to note the Custom Metal Installations case, supra. Many of the
previous decisions below may be overruled or modiﬁed by it.
Rule 9.15(4) refers to consent to reverse or vary the order. Consent merely to reargue the
merits of the order has no effect; that cannot be done.²
The power under R.9.15(4) to set aside an interlocutory order is broad, but a consent order
evidences a contract, and usually can be upset only if some of the established grounds in contract
law for attacking a contract are present.³
Rule 9.15(1) is narrow, and an entered order can be set aside only in limited circumstances;
the usual remedy is appeal.⁴
But there may be a wider power to reconsider a ﬁnding of contempt
of court.⁵
An entered interlocutory order may be later varied under R.9.15(4).⁶
Probably Rule 9.12 is not always needed to correct errors in interlocutory orders, for the court
doubtless has inherent power to vary or revoke many or most of them.⁷
An interlocutory order can
be varied if new evidence is found.⁸
Interlocutory rulings by a trial judge can be revised by her
if circumstances change.⁹
Case management judges can vary their previous procedural orders,
schedules, and litigation plans, as circumstances change, e. g. by continuing refusal to obey court
orders.¹⁰
Indeed the functus officio bar to varying entered orders or judgments may not apply to
varying their costs provisions.¹¹
Rule 9.15(4) does not apply to a true judgment, even if it is called an “order, ” such as an order
nisi of foreclosure.¹²
There is some conﬂict in the cases, and some say that even an order cannot
be set aside if it was properly given on notice and ﬁnally settles some question, such as privilege.¹³

Conversely, an order which imposes penalties (or even ends the suit) if something is not done by

R.9.17(1) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
DIVISION 4
ENFORCEMENT OF JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Enforcement: Orders for Payment and Judgments for Payment into Court
9.17(1) An order for payment may be enforced in any manner in which a judgment
for the payment of money may be enforced.¹
(2) A judgment for the payment of money into Court may be enforced in any
manner in which a judgment for the payment of money to a person may be
enforced.²
Information Note
Rule 3.7 [Post-judgment transfer of action] permits a judgment creditor to apply to the Court,
on notice to each of the other parties, for a temporary transfer of the action to a different
judicial centre for purposes of an application to enforce the judgment or order.

Related Provisions
1.4(2) (h) (stays) ; 3.7 (venue of enforcement proceedings) ; 3.37 (enforcement in another

judicial centre); 9.29 (examining a person in aid of execution) ; 10.52 (contempt) ; 13.49
(payment in to court).
A. Enforcement Generally
1. Alberta Statutory Scheme
This topic used to be called “execution”, an older term which is confusing because it has so
many different meanings.
The enforcement of judgments and the procedure for the seizure and sale of assets of a
judgment debtor in Alberta were signiﬁcantly changed when the Civil Enforcement Act³
came into
force on January 1, 1996. A hodge-podge of very old common-law remedies and procedures,
with a handful of patchwork statutory amendments, were replaced with a modern Alberta-made
scheme.
These Rules apply to the enforcement of money judgments as deﬁned in the Civil Enforcement
Act, s.1(1) (ee). Court orders, such as maintenance orders and orders to pay beneﬁts under an
automobile policy, may be enforced in the same manner as judgments.⁴
Similarly, judgments for
the payment of money into court may be enforced in the same manner as judgments for payment
of money to persons.⁵
An order obtained by a person, or an order or judgment against a person
who is not a party, may be enforced in the same manner as if the person were a party.⁶
Note R.
2.20, which addresses payments recovered by litigation representatives.

r.350 and Eng.1883 O.42 r.24.

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.17(2)
Issuing and amending writs is dealt with in the Civil Enforcement Regulation.¹
Garnishment
is dealt with in Part 1.3.²
Examination in aid of enforcement and obtaining information from the
debtor are dealt with in R.9.29.
The Civil Enforcement Regulation³
lets an enforcement creditor request conﬁrmation from
other enforcement creditors that they are dealing with the same enforcement debtor. And it lets an
enforcement creditor request from other enforcement creditors statements of the amounts owed
by the enforcement debtor to them. The request for information is made by written demand setting
out the information stipulated in the Regulation. The written demand is served on the enforcement
creditor from whom information is being requested (the “replying creditor”). The replying creditor
has 15 days from the date of service within which to reply. If the replying creditor fails to respond,
an application may be made to the court for an order directing the replying creditor to respond.
At common law, each judgment creditor could grab assets (via a bailiff) and not share them
with other unsecured creditors. The Civil Enforcement Act does some things to require sharing,
but where the Crown manages to collect a debt through other kinds of proceedings under another
enactment, they do not have to share.⁴
On application by an interested party or agency, the court may give directions concerning
enforcement proceedings under the Civil Enforcement Act.⁵
2. Stays
(a) Possible Grounds
The court has a discretion not to enforce execution.⁶
And a discretion to withhold a stay, e. g.
from a penalty for continuing contempt and deﬁance of court orders.⁷
An appeal does not itself operate as a stay of execution.⁸
The court may give an order to
stay the enforcement of a judgment or remove or extend a stay already granted in respect of a
judgment.⁹
The appellant may apply for a stay ﬁrst to the justice appealed from and then if the stay
is refused, apply to a judge of the Court of Appeal.¹⁰
A stay of execution can be given more generally if not pending appeal, under a contract, or the
Judicature Act s.17, or under the court’s inherent power. One ground for a stay of execution is a
plausible counterclaim or set-off.¹¹
But the latter does not apply where the judgment debtor’s cross-
claims are against not only the judgment creditor, but a considerable number of other persons,
and so possibly might never get a judgment against the judgment creditor. That is not a plausible
counterclaim or set-off.

9-52
R.9.17(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
(b) Tests
A stay of execution pending appeal is not a message that the appeal has merit, as the “serious
question” test is a low bar.¹
The test that is applied on an application for a stay of execution pending appeal is generally
that which is applied on an application for an injunction:²
(i) there is a serious question raised;³
(ii) the applicant will suffer irreparable harm if the application is refused;⁴
and (iii) the balance of
convenience favours the granting of the stay.⁵
In applying this test, the court will consider whether
enforcement of the judgment will render a successful appeal nugatory. The 3 parts of the test are
not water tight compartments, and public interest relates to the third stage (balance), not to the
second (irreparable harm).⁶
The core test is usually the balance of convenience, partly because
it is only at this stage (part of the test) that harm to the respondent opposing the stay application,
is examined by the judge, and only at this stage that the judge looks at relevant strength of the
appellant’s case, and relative likelihood of irreparable harm.⁷
A stay may be granted on conditions.⁸
Even though the tests for a stay were not made out, the respondent was ordered not to argue
in the Court of Appeal or in the court below, that the appellant’s participation was attornment to
the jurisdiction.⁹

9-53
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.17(2)
A stay of an order to sell property may be denied if any loss or expenses would not be
irreparable. And if the applicant seeking the stay had deﬁed three court orders and lived rent-free
on the property, he lacked clean hands.¹
(c) Miscellaneous
It is relevant how quickly the appeal can be heard. After 4 years’ delay, more delay is unfortunate,
but a few more months may not be too much.²
That was more so when the party seeking the stay
offered to undertake that no one would take steps against the respondents in the interim, and that
the respondent could add any difference in interest rates payable by the two sides.
On transitional legislation and whether an automatic stay falls within the words “granted a
stay”, see the Medovarski case.³
A stay of execution by an Alberta judge under the federal Supreme Court Act requires good
proof of an intent to apply to the Supreme Court of Canada for leave to appeal, especially if for
weeks the applicant has not done so.⁴
An order granting or extending a stay in enforcement proceedings under the Civil Enforcement
Act must be registered in the Personal Property Registry, or the order will not affect persons who
do not have actual knowledge of it.
If preserving a company in business and getting its debts paid in a while is not possible, then
a Companies’ Creditors Arrangement Act proceeding is in effect a liquidation, which s.36 (since
2009) has allowed. The company may then survive, albeit in a restructured form and less some
important assets or parts of its business. But if the company will never survive or emerge, then
liquidation is what is going on, and the objectives differ.⁵
If there is concern that a judgment debtor has, or will likely dispose of or destroy or dissipate
exigible assets, the court may give an order that the debtor post security for the amount of the
judgment. But it takes exceptional circumstances.⁶
Stay of future proceedings (not just of execution of a past order) entails dangers. If guilt has
been found, staying any penalty, argument, or decision tends to lead to litigation by instalments,
which has many evils and is usually discouraged.⁷
The usual test for such a stay is the 3-part one
(like that for stay of execution), and if that test is not satisﬁed, a special interlocutory one would
be needed, but it is rare.⁸
3. Procedure
The Civil Enforcement Regulation⁹
prescribes rules for the service of documents under the
Civil Enforcement Act.¹⁰
Note that though a document sent by prepaid registered mail is deemed

9-54
R.9.17(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
to have been served when the addressee signs for the document or 7 days from the date the
document is mailed,¹
it is open to the court to ﬁnd that the addressee did not receive the document
and to make an appropriate order.²
Both Sections 35.05(9) and 35.06(2) provide that they do
not prevent documents from being served in any other manner permitted under the Rules.³
Section 35.07 allows service on a person who has consented in writing, before or after the date
of judgment, by a method other than the methods described.
An assignee of a judgment can enforce it against the judgment debtors, even if the assignee
is himself a defendant, and has no other claim against the judgment debtors, so long as he is not
a co-surety of theirs.⁴
Respondents ordered to pay money who had assets to do so refused to pay, and took various
legal steps to stall enforcement. Finally a receiver was appointed. As the then Rules said “the
conduct of any party that tended to shorten or to unnecessarily lengthen the proceeding” was
relevant, the court ordered a multiple of the usual Schedule C costs, a bit over a straight multiplier
of 3.⁵
A receiver appointed in the wake of an alleged fraudulent Ponzi scheme has standing to apply
for an attachment order against those alleged to have received the missing money.⁶
An agreement between the sheriff’s ofﬁce and various civil enforcement agencies (bailiffs)
in Alberta sets the tariff of fees which the agencies can charge. They cannot charge more. An
assessment ofﬁcer cannot review the account of such an agency, even if it is brought in to the
ofﬁcer with a bill of costs.⁷
Legislation allows the Labour Relations Board to ﬁle one of its decisions in the Court of King’s
Bench, which ﬁling would then allow the decision to be enforced like a court judgment. The Board
had given an order directing access at certain work sites, and the union wanted that order to
be ﬁled with the court. Meanwhile, a settlement of the dispute had been reached. The Board
therefore refused to ﬁle its decision with the court, and that was held reasonable.⁸
When a tribunal decides the merits and that is afﬁrmed by the Court of Appeal, that is ﬁnal. In
later proceedings about how to enforce the earlier decision, the merits can no longer be argued.
New evidence is admissible on appeal from the later enforcement decision only if it is relevant to
a legitimate issue about enforcement. Fresh evidence about the original merits is irrelevant and
inadmissible.⁹
Where misconduct is shown, but it falls short of proving contempt, the court still has power to
make necessary orders, to achieve justice or maintain conﬁdentiality.¹⁰
Repeated stay applications, especially after the orders sought to be stayed had already been
afﬁrmed by the Court of Appeal, led to higher costs, especially as the amounts claimed were so
high.¹¹

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.17(2)
A purported Provincial Court civil judgment was ﬁled in the Court of King’s Bench for
enforcement, but it was later found to be a forgery, there being no such judgment or even lawsuit.
So a Justice of the King’s Bench ordered that the new King’s Bench ﬁle be immediately vacated
nunc pro tunc, and a formal order doing that was to be prepared at once.¹
4. Arbitration Awards
Courts must enforce arbitral awards, with a few narrow statutory exceptions, such as an appeal
or setting aside.²
The court cannot amend the award. Except maybe for lack of court jurisdiction
to give that remedy, or a remedy contrary to Alberta public policy.³
The court may give a different
remedy, or send remedy back to the arbitrator to reconsider, a remedy it could not grant. The court
enforcing an award has the same powers as it does to enforce its own judgments. An arbitral
award is more than a binding legal text; it is an adjudicated disposition, similar to a court order or
judgment. Enforcement applications are not a chance to relitigate what the arbitrator has decided,
except with an application under ss.49(3), (4) of the Arbitration Act.⁴
B. Garnishment
1. Introduction
Though all previous N. W. T. and Alberta sets of Rules of Court dealt with garnishment, the

Footnote
2010 Rules do not. That legislation has been moved from the Rules to ss.35.19 to 35.31 of the
Civil Enforcement Regulation. That Regulation is reproduced in v.3 of this book. Parts 3 and 8 of
the Civil Enforcement Act also govern garnishment. The Regulation merely supplements Part 8
of the Act.
Garnishment is a way of forcing a debtor (called the garnishee) to pay the money which he
or she owes, but pay it to the court, instead of paying it to his or her creditor. That is done when
his or her creditor is or may become a judgment debtor. Usually the garnishee is a stranger to
the lawsuit. The Clerk of the Court issues a document called a Garnishee Summons,⁵
it is served
on the garnishee, and attaches the debt in question. If the garnishee thereafter pays his or her
original creditor, the debt is not discharged, and must be paid again to the court.⁶
But if the
garnishee pays into court, then his or her debt is (to that extent) discharged.⁷
For example, Smith gets a judgment against Jones, which Jones does not pay. But Jones has
money in a bank account, which means the bank owes the amount of the bank account balance
to Jones. Smith issues a garnishee summons against the bank, requiring it to pay money to the
court instead of to Jones.
It is doubtful that garnishment is a “seizure” under the Charter, and it is also doubtful that s.8
of the Charter applies to procedures to enforce civil judgments (or tax assessments).⁸
1 JCI Filtration & Separation v. Rohrich 2022 ABKB 660, JCC 2201 04163 (Oct 4).
2 Gouthro v. Gouthro, infra (¶’s 21-22, 25, 39, 42, 43; cf. ¶ 44).
3 Gouthro v. Gouthro, infra (¶’s 39-40).
4 Gouthro v. Gouthro 2024 ABKB 232, JCC 4801 184517 (Mar 12).
5 Form L to the Rules.
6 There is an exception for a payment in good faith a short time after service: see R.475.
7 See the Civil Enforcement Act, s.78(h), and the C. P. E. , Chapter 58, Part V.
8 Even if the Charter is triggered, the legislation is reasonable and justiﬁable under the Charter. Nor do the
provisions making such debts prior to other debts, and barring their garnishment in turn for other debts,
infringe the constitutional guarantee of independence of the judiciary: Millar v. Millar 2000 ABCA 100, 255
AR 141. See also C. P. E. , Chap.58, Part K.6.

9-56
R.9.17(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
2. Garnishment Before Judgment
(a) Introduction
When garnishment is effected after judgment as a means of enforcing that judgment, the
procedure is straightforward.
But garnishment is sometimes available before judgment as well (e. g. when Smith has sued
Jones but not yet got judgment against Jones). That is now effected by getting an attachment
order under Part 3 of the Civil Enforcement Act.¹
That can extend much beyond attaching debts,
indeed to something much like a Mareva injunction.²
(b) The Tests
No longer is the plaintiff conﬁned to a liquidated claim; she may be suing for damages. The
basic tests for attachment are set out in s.17(2) :
(i) the defendant must be doing something outside normal business or living which is likely
seriously to hinder the plaintiff in enforcing any later judgment;³
and
(ii) the plaintiff must show a reasonable likelihood that she will win the suit.⁴
If the court denies pretrial attachment, it may instead award security for costs against the
defendant.⁵
An Applications Judge may give such an order.⁶
If a plaintiff seeking an attachment order before trial gives some evidence of the facts for its
cause of action, and is not really cross-examined and does not meet any contrary evidence, the
plaintiff must have the arguable cause of action (e. g. strong prima facie case) based on that.
The chambers judge cannot assume that there will be contrary evidence at trial, still less strong
evidence, and must take the evidence of the plaintiff at face value.⁷
The court has a discretion to refuse a pre-trial attachment order.⁸
On how an afﬁdavit satisﬁes test (ii), see the C. P. E.⁹
The new test (i) is tougher than the old
tests for prejudgment garnishment, and the cases on that decided before the Civil Enforcement
Act are no longer reliable. Instead cases on Mareva injunctions may be more useful.¹⁰
To get a
Mareva or similar injunction, it is not enough to show a reasonable likelihood that the plaintiff’s
claim will succeed. The applicant must also show either some evidence of the defendant’s wrongful

assets can be inferred: Lion’s Gate Homes v. Bahramloian, infra. A pretrial attachment order is improper
where the “oppression” complained of is expressly permitted by the company’s Articles: Ziegler Est. v.
Green Acres (Pine L. ) 2011 ABCA 295, 519 AR 138.

Ent. v. Bergen (#2) 2000 ABQB 963, 282 AR 180.

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.17(2)
dissipation or disposition of assets, or likely inability of the defendants to pay any judgment
awarded to the plaintiff. A mere expression of fear is not enough evidence.¹
Ordering that funds
due be held in trust once paid is tantamount to pre-judgment execution, and the tests for it are just
as stiff.²
Balance of convenience is relevant, though it is only one of the tests.³
(c) Procedure and Flaws
The courts used to take a narrow interpretation of who could swear the afﬁdavit to get
garnishment before judgment, and there is some danger that those cases may still apply.⁴
Presumably the old law on what to put in an afﬁdavit for garnishee summons after judgment will
still largely apply.⁵
On the scope of cross-examination on an afﬁdavit ﬁled to set aside an order for
pre-judgment attachment, see the MacDonald case.⁶
Garnishment used to be cursed with court decisions which took an extremely picky and
technical reading of the Rules or legislation, and held trivial, even clerical, defects to be fatal. That
is clearly wrong, because of R.1.5. Such old precedents should be buried now. Some of the more
tolerant cases curing or ignoring defects may still be useful.⁷
Another important change in the law is that a plaintiff seeking any prejudgment attachment now
must give an undertaking as to damages, similar to one given to get an interlocutory injunction.⁸
No relief was given to the plaintiff during an adjournment (for cross-examination) of a motion
for pre-trial attachment, because of apprehended harm to the defendant’s reputation.⁹
An attachment order given before trial will expire 60 days after entry of any judgment, even
a partial judgment. In any event, a plaintiff getting an attachment order must move the suit along
promptly; delays of 6 months or a year are unacceptable. Especially if the attachment was got
primarily because fraud was alleged, but it is other parts of the suit on which the plaintiff is moving
forward.¹⁰
3. Effects of Garnishment
Service of the garnishee summons upon an “afﬁliate” of a bank rather than its head ofﬁce
postpones the effective date of the garnishment.¹¹

R.9.17(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Under the old law, garnishment was often ineffective because it could only attach debts
which were already due at the time of service of the garnishee summons, or were then accruing
due. And “accruing” had a technical and narrow meaning.¹
Now under ss.77 and 78 of the Civil
Enforcement Act (unless otherwise ordered), a garnishee summons attaches any duty to pay
money which is payable either
* already, or
* on demand, or
* on personal application, or on presentation of a document, or
* in the future or in certain circumstances or at a certain time under an existing agreement
or trust, the will of a deceased, or shares or other marketable securities, or
* in the ordinary course of events under a present employment, or
* (likely) under statute and past events, or
* (possibly) under an existing cause of action.
So the old cases on what sums could not be garnished should be approached with extreme
caution; they may well be totally inapplicable. Needless to say, the garnishee need not actually
pay until the due date arrives. A garnishment served on a bank or other deposit institution is good
for 60 days. Other garnishments are good for two years, but may be withdrawn sooner, or may be
renewed for successive years.²
If there is an order allowing prejudgment attachment by garnishment, there is no reason that
the resulting garnishee summons has to expire when the order authorizing it does, though it may
be that the summons must be issued before the order expires. And a garnishee summons is a
valid court command until set aside.³
On a motion to set aside garnishment, the question is not
whether what was done before was right, but whether (on the evidence now available) it is now
just to have attachment before judgment.⁴
Most old cases said that joint debts were not subject to garnishment where only one creditor
was a judgment debtor, but the Civil Enforcement Act and the Maintenance Enforcement Act now
reverse that.⁵
The Civil Enforcement Act presumes that an equal share belongs to each creditor.⁶
Deposit of a cheque into a joint account may give the bank rights of a holder, but does not give the
Minister of National Revenue the right to garnish moneys in that account because of a debt owing
by only one of the joint owners of the account, even if he is the one who deposited the cheque.⁷
An Alberta court cannot attach assets which are not in Alberta (assuming that there is no
relevant and valid federal legislation authorizing that). So the old cases on territorial jurisdiction
likely still apply.⁸
One big exception to garnishment is an obligation evidenced by a cheque or other bill of
exchange, by a document transferable on delivery, or by a letter of credit or credit advice which

Footnote
1 Especially as Alberta has no general apportionment or accrual legislation and the common law does not
usually so provide.
2 See s.79.
3 Cameron v. Aecometric Corp. (CA 1998) 212 AR 368, 60 Alta LR(3d) 129.
4 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 58, Part I.2.

6 s.82(d) ; but the Maintenance Enforcement Act does not: Sorenson v. Dir. , supra.

8 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 58, Part F.

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.17(2)
must be surrendered to be paid. Such debts cannot be garnished.¹
The distinction between them
and deposit accounts in s.83(1) is likely to be troublesome.
4. Exemptions
Another big exception to garnishment (as before 2010), is exemptions from wage garnishment.
One cannot attach all of a person’s wages. They are governed by s.81, and the actual amounts
of the exempt and garnishable parts are set by Regulation.²
The Civil Enforcement Act deﬁnes
employment earnings broadly, and so it includes severance pay on termination.³
An exemption of “sums paid or reimbursed” from a certain source merely exempts from seizure
the right to receive such sums; it does not exempt the funds from seizure after they have been
paid and put into another account.⁴
If funds are exempt from seizure, their transfer cannot be
attacked as a fraudulent conveyance.⁵
Many other statutes⁶
render certain assets exempt from attachment or execution, such as
certain pensions.⁷
Garnishment under federal tax legislation is expressly subject to restrictions on
execution in the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act, which incorporates provincial exemptions. So the
ﬁrst $40, 000 proceeds of the sale of a home are exempt.⁸
Some forms of execution, such as garnishment, used to apply only to certain types of property,
especially before the Civil Enforcement Act. But that is not an exemption from execution, and so
lawyers’ trust accounts or retainers in them are not always exempt property under the Bankruptcy
and Insolvency Act.⁹
Though there is Crown immunity from execution, if a case can be decided without recourse
to such immunity, other grounds should be resorted to. One alternative is paramountcy of federal
legislation over provincial. Even if there is no operational conﬂict between federal and provincial
legislation because one is permissive and one is prohibitive, there can still be a conﬂict of
purposes. A “requirement to pay” in federal law prevailed over Quebec legislation exempting from
seizure income replacement beneﬁts.¹⁰
5. Amount Garnished
Garnishment cannot be for an amount much more than the total of all applicable judgments by
various creditors against the judgment debtor, or the likely amount of the plaintiff’s claim (in the
case of attachment before judgment).

R.9.17(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
On garnishment of money jointly owed by the judgment debtor and a non-party, see the
Merchant case.¹
As under previous legislation, moneys secured by garnishment must ordinarily be distributed
among all those judgment creditors entitled.²
That is why one judgment creditor can garnish for
the total of all the unsatisﬁed judgments applicable. But a writ of enforcement by another judgment
creditor must be ﬁled quickly, lest it be too late to share in moneys paid into court under another
creditor’s garnishment.³
On a request to adjust a continuing garnishment, the judgment debtor has a right to be heard
by the court.⁴
A judgment creditor can enforce (execute) at once, even if the judgment debt is
costs.⁵
There can be wage garnishment for family maintenance and also for ordinary debts, at the
same time, so long as the total exemptions available are ﬁrst respected.⁶
6. Where Garnishee Does Not Pay
If a garnishee complies with the Regulation’s ss.35.23 and 35.24 and disputes generally its
liability to the debtor, it need not give any more explanation.⁷
If the garnishee does not pay into court when it should, judgment or other relief may be given
against the garnishee.⁸
The older cases on that subject are likely still applicable.⁹
If the garnishee
ﬁles an appearance and says (inter alia) that the money in question is not a debt, the creditor
plaintiff’s only remedy is trial of an issue; he cannot move for judgment against the garnishee.¹⁰
A garnishee did not pay anything into court, and denied liability to the bankrupt. The time
deadlines had been tight, and there had been timely notice of the existence of new evidence, and
the substance of the information had not been known.¹¹
The proposed additional evidence was
underlying cross-examination already in evidence, and so useful for the court to understand it.
7. Priorities
There is a lot of case law on priorities, e. g. a contest between garnishment and an earlier
unserved assignment of the debt garnished. The Civil Enforcement Act is designed to introduce
a new priority régime tied into the Personal Property Security Act,¹²
so older cases on priorities

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.18(1)
should be approached with extreme caution. But if an assignment is absolute and unconditional
and predates service of the garnishee summons, there is nothing left to attach, and the assignment
prevails.¹
Trusts and trust conditions are valid, and cannot be ignored when seeing if any money is
owing to be garnished.²
The same is true of matrimonial property and suits for its division.³
8. Miscellaneous
Which province or country’s courts have jurisdiction to issue a garnishee summons (or
garnishment order) in respect of a debt depends upon to whom the debt is owed. If it is owed to
a corporate body (here a U. N. agency) domiciled in the province ordering garnishment, the court
has jurisdiction, because a debt is usually situated where it is collectible, the domicile or principal
place of business of the debtor of the debt.⁴
It is not conclusive that the debtor and the creditor
agreed that sums were payable in another country, as that is res inter alios acta not binding the
creditor or the debtor.⁵
The garnishment is about attaching an ordinary unsecured debt, even if
that debt arose because of dealings with money or other property.⁶
So in rem rights are irrelevant.⁷
A debt is a debt for garnishment purposes, whatever the precise relationship between the debtor
and creditor of that debt.⁸
A tax garnishment (“requirement to pay”) should not be served on the Clerk of the Court in
respect of moneys in court, and that could be contempt, even if it is intended as mere notice, not
as compulsion. The Clerk has a duty to pay out only as directed by King’s Bench orders, and so
is not “liable to make a payment. ” The proper remedy may be a motion on notice under R.6.27.⁹
Income Tax Act garnishment (over employee withholdings in a deemed trust by employer) was
held to prevail over claims of builders’ liens. The money in question had been paid into court as
security to get the liens off title.¹⁰
Judgments and Orders Subject to Conditions
9.18(1) If a judgment or order is made subject to conditions that a party must
fulﬁl, a party to whom the conditions apply may not do anything further to
enforce the judgment or order until
(a) the party has ﬁled an afﬁdavit conﬁrming that the conditions have
been met, or
(b) the Court so permits.

If a Rule (such as 10.4) requires proof of some fact to get relief, the judge may give such relief
conditional on proof of that fact before the order becomes enforceable.¹
Persons Who are Not Parties
9.19 If a person is not a party to an action but
(a) the person obtains an order or an order is obtained in the person’s
favour, the person may enforce the order in the same manner as if
the person were a party to the action, or
(b) the person is subject to a judgment or order granted in respect of that
action, the judgment or order may be enforced against the person in
the same manner as if the person were a party to the action.²

Related Provisions
2.20 (litigation representatives) ; 6.4 (injunctions) ; 10.52 (contempt) ; 13.45 (orders imposing

duties on the clerk).
Time Writ Remains in Force
9.20 Unless an enactment otherwise provides, and except for the purpose of the
enactment, a writ remains in force as long as the judgment or order under which
the writ was issued is in force.³

Related Provisions
3.7 (venue of enforcement proceedings) ; 6.53 (expiry of replevin order).

A. References
Issuing and amending writs of enforcement is dealt with in the Civil Enforcement Act and the
Civil Enforcement Regulation, Part 1.1.
B. Writs of Enforcement
A writ of enforcement now replaces the former writ of execution.

Footnote
1 Paragon Cap. Corp. v. Starke Dom.2020 ABCA 216, 6 Alta LR(7th) 253.
2 Quite similar to previous R.346. It came from 1968 R.357, which was virtually identical to 1914 R.599, and

9-63
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.20
Where a judgment grants relief subject to conditions, on fulﬁlment of those conditions the
party entitled to the relief may apply to the court for leave to issue a writ.¹
Where judgment has
been granted without conditions, the Clerk of the Court may be required by a judgment creditor
to issue a writ of enforcement at any time that the judgment is in force, provided that any period
for payment speciﬁed in the judgment has passed.²
A judgment is not in force, for the purposes
of determining whether a writ is in force, if the judgment has been satisﬁed, or if 10 years have
elapsed from the date the judgment was entered with the Clerk of the Court, unless the judgment
has been renewed or an action was brought on the judgment within that 10 year period.³
C. Various Deadlines
Once issued by the Clerk of the Court, a writ of enforcement must be registered in the Personal
Property Registry, at which time the writ may be enforced throughout Alberta without regard to
judicial districts or centres.⁴
Rule 3.7 allows enforcement other than by writ to take place at a
different judicial centre. A judgment creditor may not initiate writ proceedings in relation to a
money judgment against personal property unless the writ is registered in the Personal Property
Registry; or, against land, unless the writ is registered under the Land Titles Act⁵
against the
certiﬁcate of title to the land.⁶
Subject to legislative enactment, a writ remains in force as long as the judgment remains in
force.⁷
But registration of the writ in the Personal Property Registry is only effective for 2 years
from the date of registration. It may be renewed in accordance with the Personal Property Security
Act.⁸
Registration of a writ under the Land Titles Act is effective as long as the judgment remains
in force.⁹
On computing limitation periods where an Alberta Court of Justice judgment was ﬁled in
the Court of King’s Bench, see the Zavers case,¹⁰
which mentions the Covid-19 extension.
The writ of enforcement must be in the form set by the Minister.¹¹
D. Amount Enforceable
The amount owing at any time on a writ is the amount of the judgment plus costs not included
in the judgment amount, and interest owing on the judgment and the costs, less any amounts paid
to the judgment creditor in respect of the judgment.¹²
The creditor is required to register a status
report at the Personal Property Registry amending the amount owing under a writ within 15 days
of receiving a payment towards or in satisfaction of amount owing under the writ.¹³
Except as otherwise provided in the Civil Enforcement Act or other legislation, proceedings
by way of writ of enforcement are taken on behalf of or for the beneﬁt of all enforcement creditors
(persons in whose favour writs are in force) against the enforcement debtor (a person against
whom a writ is in force. )¹⁴

1 Rule 9.18.
2 Civil Enforcement Act, s.25.1.
3 Civil Enforcement Act, s.27(2).
4 Civil Enforcement Act, s.2(c). Alias writs and ﬁlings at the sheriff’s ofﬁce are no longer required.

9-64
R.9.21(1) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
E. Enforcement Agencies
Licensed civil enforcement agencies now carry out seizures, evictions, sale of seized property,
and the distribution of sale proceeds.¹
When the writ of enforcement has been issued by the Clerk
of the Court and registered in the Personal Property Registry, the judgment creditor may instruct
a civil enforcement agency to effect the seizure of all exigible assets of the enforcement debtor.²
F. Miscellaneous
Without court order, the Clerk may issue or amend a writ to name properly a judgment creditor
whose name has appeared incorrectly on a judgment or writ, or has changed.³
But a court order
is required to amend a writ or to issue a writ where the name of the judgment debtor that appears
on the judgment is not the proper name.⁴
A judgment creditor may assign a writ to another person, and the Clerk is authorized to amend
a writ to show the name of the assignee, without court order.⁵
If there is only a partial assignment
of the judgment creditor’s rights under the writ, the Clerk may issue replacement writs, dated
with the same date as the original writ, to the judgment creditor and the assignee setting out the
amounts owing to each of them.
Application for New Judgment or Order
9.21(1) On application, the Court may grant a judgment creditor a new judgment
or order on a former judgment or any part of it that has not been paid.
(2) [Repealed Alta. Reg.128/15]
(3) Notice of the application must
(a) be ﬁled before the expiry of the limitation period under the Limitations
Act for an action on the judgment, and
(b) be served on the judgment debtor by the same method by which a
commencement document must be served.
(4) An application under this rule is an application in the original action.
(5) If the judgment debtor does not appear at the hearing of the application, and
show cause why a new judgment or order should be denied, the Court may grant
the judgment creditor a new judgment or order for the amount due and a costs
award if the Court is satisﬁed that
(a) notice of the application was served on the judgment debtor, and
(b) the amount has not been paid under the original judgment or order.

or in part, the Court may

The method of service for commencement documents (see rule 9.21(3) (b) ) is set out in Part

Footnote

procedural order
Related Provisions
3.2(3) (application in an action) ; 6.53 (expiry of replevin order) ; 7.1 (trial of issues) ; 13.5

(varying time).
At common law, one could sue on a judgment at any time, and get another similar judgment.
That may sound like a waste of time, but it had its uses, including suits here on foreign judgments.
Courts usually hold that enforcing a judgment is subject to the usual limitation periods,²
and
many limitations statutes set an express time limit for that.³
However, if one sues before that time
expires,⁴
one can get a new judgment, and the time to enforce it runs from its date, not from the
date of the original judgment.⁵
For the purpose of calculating the ten-year limitation period for
renewing a judgment, one looks at the actual date of judgment, not the slightly earlier date of
noting the defendant in default.⁶
Rule 9.21 has its own 10-year time limit, so R.4.33 does not
apply to it.⁷
A partition and sale order does not cease to have effect after 10 years under the Civil
Enforcement Act, as that applies only to money judgments.⁸
Rule 9.21 allows a simpler procedure to renew the original judgment rather than issuing a new
statement of claim.⁹
In case of doubt about time limits, it might be safer to issue a new statement
of claim suing on the old judgment (not on the original cause of action).¹⁰
Choosing to sue on the
original judgment does not open it up so that the defendant could then plead the merits. The
previous judgment makes that res judicata. That is doubly so when the original judgment is

2 For limitations purposes, references to “judgments” mean domestic judgments; foreign judgments or

AR 372, affd (SCC) 2010 SCC 19, 401 NR 236.
3 See Limitation Act RSA 2000 c. L-12 s.11.
4 A creditor is not obliged to wait until the judgment has almost expired in order to remove to renew it, and
it is proper to do it two years “early” so that all the judgments in the same matter will have the same dates:
Turkawski v.738675 Alta.2008 ABQB 313, 445 AR 9.
5 Discussion of various forms of acknowledgment or part payment as extending the life of a judgment: Vienne
v. Fraslin (Alta CA) [1921] 3 WWR 600, 62 DLR 647.
6 Agric. Finl. Serv. Corp. v. Zaborski 2013 ABCA 277, 556 AR 180. On the Alberta Court of Justice judgments
ﬁled in the Court of King’s Bench, see R.9.20 n.
7 Alta. Treas. Br. v. Cdn. Egg Processing (M) 2014 ABQB 548, 596 AR 373.

R.9.22(1) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
based on default, so that the defendant is deemed to admit material allegations in the statement
of claim.¹
The renewed judgment cannot include interest in the old judgment, as that would lead to
compound interest.²
It is doubtful that a judgment creditor has any duty to enforce his judgment;
the debtor has a duty to pay.³
On a motion to renew a costs order (after almost ten years), if the evidence of non-payment
is not clear enough, an Applications Judge may adjourn the motion and allow the party moving to
ﬁle a further better afﬁdavit.⁴
The duty is on the defendant to pay a judgment, not on the plaintiff to
enforce it; so the defendant must show cause why it should not be renewed.⁵
A request in Bankruptcy Act proceedings to say that a judgment existing does or does not
have certain consequences, is not a request for a “remedial order” saving a limitation period.
Usually for purposes of R.9.21(3) such words mean an action on the judgment. So the creditors
may lose all beneﬁt of their judgment, without notice that there is a bankruptcy, nor a chance to do
anything about that before there is a discharge. In theory the creditors could have ﬁled a claim or
renewal application concurrently with the one which they did ﬁle.⁶
If a new judgment must be served on the judgment debtor by the same method by which a
commencement document must be served (R.9.21(3) ), and the debtor is a corporation struck
off the register years before, so service on the last registered ofﬁce of the dissolved corporation
sufﬁces.⁷
Application That Judgment or Order has Been Satisﬁed
9.22(1) On application, the Court may make an order that a judgment or order
has been satisﬁed.
(2) The application must
(a) be in Form 41,
(b) be ﬁled, and
(c) be served on the affected parties by the same method by which a
commencement document must be served.
(3) The court clerk must include in the court ﬁle a memorandum that a judgment
or order has been satisﬁed if
(a) the Court so orders, or
(b) the judgment creditor or the judgment creditor’s lawyer acknowledges
in writing that the judgment or order has been satisﬁed.

Enforcement Against Partners’ and Partnership Property
9.23(1) A judgment or order against a partnership in the partnership name may
be enforced against the partnership’s property.
(2) A judgment or order against a partnership in the partnership name may also
be enforced, if the judgment or order or a subsequent judgment or order so
provides, against a person who was served with a notice under rule 2.3 [Suing
individual partners] and who, at the time speciﬁed in the notice,
(a) is presumed to be a partner under rule 2.3 [Suing individual partners],
(b) admits to being a partner, or
(c) is adjudged to be a partner.
(3) If after a judgment or order has been made against a partnership in the
partnership name the party obtaining it claims to be entitled to enforce it against
a person alleged to be a partner who was not served in accordance with rule
2.3(1) [Suing individual partners], the party may apply to the Court to enforce the
order or judgment against the alleged partner, and even though no notice was
served under rule 2.3(1), the Court may permit the party to enforce the judgment
or order
(a) if liability of the person as a partner is not disputed, or
(b) if liability is disputed, after the liability has been determined in the
manner directed by the Court.⁴

5 (U. K. ), on application by the judgment creditor, the Court may order property

(a) the judgment debtor, and

Information Note
The application referred to in this rule must be made in an action started by statement of
claim.

An application to set aside a conveyance within a year of bankruptcy can be based on s.3 of
the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act, though if it is outside a year, part of the relief must be setting
aside the fraudulent transaction.²
A motion under Rule 9.24 giving sufﬁcient particulars can be considered the commencement
of an action, for limitations purposes.³

was not struck.⁴

the conveyance is fraudulent, and so creditors can get full value at the time of their later judgment.⁵

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.25(1)

Order of Possession of Land
9.25(1) This rule applies when a judgment or order of possession orders a person
to give up possession of land to another person.
(2) A judgment or order of possession must include a statement to the effect
that a civil enforcement agency has authority, after service of the order has been
effected, to evict any occupant of the land.
(3) Unless the Court otherwise orders, the judgment or order of possession must
be served on every person ordered to give up possession of the land and every
occupant of the land.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;143/11]
Information Note
The Court may impose terms and conditions on its orders under rule 1.4 [Procedural orders].

On orders for possession, see also Residential Tenancies Act.³
Authority to Evict Occupants
9.26(1) A civil enforcement agency has authority to evict a person from land the
person occupies
(a) only in accordance with a judgment or order of possession, and
(b) unless the Court otherwise orders, only after the civil enforcement
agency is satisﬁed that
(i) the judgment or order has been served on every occupant of
the land, and
(ii) an afﬁdavit of service has been ﬁled by the person who has
the judgment or order of possession.
(2) If a judgment or order of possession is stayed while payments are being made
in accordance with the judgment or order granting the stay, the judgment or
order of possession may not, unless the Court otherwise orders, subsequently
be enforced until notice of default is served on every person ordered to give up
possession of the land and every occupant of the land.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;143/11]

R.9.27(1) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
Removal, Storage and Sale of Personal Property
9.27(1) A person who enforces a judgment or order of possession in respect of
premises need not remove personal property from the premises.
(2) If a civil enforcement agency removes and stores any personal property
in connection with the enforcement of a judgment or order of possession, the
owner of the personal property may, on the written authorization of the civil
enforcement agency, obtain the personal property from storage by
(a) paying to the civil enforcement agency the costs, including
transportation and storage costs, that were paid by the civil
enforcement agency or by the person on whose behalf the judgment
or order of possession was enforced, and
(b) paying to the person storing the personal property any further
outstanding storage charges.
(3) If personal property is sold in accordance with an order made under an
enactment, the proceeds of sale must be applied as follows:
(a) ﬁrst, to pay the costs of the sale;
(b) second, to pay storage, transportation and other costs incurred in
removing and storing the personal property and ﬁling the application
for the order for sale;
(c) third, unless the Court otherwise orders or an enactment otherwise
provides, to pay the balance to the owner of the personal property.¹

Abandoned Goods
9.28(1) In this rule,
(a) “abandoned goods” means personal property left on land or at
premises by a person who has
(i) been evicted from the land or premises by a civil enforcement
agency, or
(ii) vacated the land or premises as a result of a judgment or
order of possession;
(b) “judgment holder” means a person who has a judgment or order of
possession.
(2) A judgment holder may dispose of abandoned goods if the judgment holder
believes on reasonable grounds that the abandoned goods have a market value
of less than $2000.
(3) Even though abandoned goods have a market value of $2000 or more, a
judgment holder may sell the goods by a means and for a price that the judgment

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous 1968? R.363(1), (2), (4). It was ﬁrst enacted effective Jan.1, 1996.

9-71
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.28(4)
holder believes is reasonable if the judgment holder believes on reasonable
grounds that
(a) storing the abandoned goods would be unsanitary or unsafe or
would rapidly result in total or substantial depreciation of the market
value of the goods, or
(b) the cost of removing, storing and selling the goods would exceed
the proceeds of their sale.
(4) If subrules (2) and (3) do not apply, the judgment holder
(a) must store or arrange for storage of the abandoned goods on behalf
of the owner or person entitled to possession for one month after
the date of their abandonment, and
(b) after the one month has expired, may dispose of the goods by public
auction or, with the approval of the Court, by private sale.
(5) If no bid is received for the abandoned goods at a public auction held under
subrule (4) (b), the judgment holder may dispose of the goods.
(6) No liability attaches to a person for
(a) selling abandoned goods under subrule (3) or (4) (b), or
(b) disposing of abandoned goods under subrule (2) or (5).
(7) If abandoned goods are disposed of or sold under this rule, the person
acquiring the goods on the disposal or sale acquires the interest of the owner or
person entitled to possess those goods, and the interest of any other person in
those goods is extinguished.
(8) A judgment holder must give up possession of abandoned goods to the
person entitled to them on payment of the costs of removing and storing them.
(9) A judgment holder
(a) may apply the proceeds of any sale of abandoned goods
(i) to the judgment holder’s costs of removing, storing and
selling the goods, and
(ii) to satisfy the judgment debtor’s liability to the judgment
holder,
and
(b) must pay the surplus of the proceeds of sale after payment under
clause (a), if any, to the Crown in right of Alberta.
(10) The President of Treasury Board and Minister of Finance must retain the
surplus on behalf of the judgment debtor for one year and, if the judgment
debtor has not claimed it within the one year period, must pay the surplus into
the General Revenue Fund.
(11) On payment of the surplus into the General Revenue Fund the judgment
debtor’s claim to the surplus is extinguished.

R.9.28(12) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
(12) A judgment holder must keep a record of the storage and disposition or sale
of abandoned goods under this rule, including
(a) a description of the goods,
(b) the period for which the goods were stored and the location,
(c) when subrule (8) applies, the costs claimed by the judgment holder
and the date on which the goods were returned to the person entitled
to them,
(d) if the goods are sold, the particulars of the sale, the amount claimed
by the judgment holder under subrule (9) and the amount of the
surplus, if any, paid to the Crown under this rule, and
(e) if the goods are neither returned to the person entitled to them nor
sold, the manner in which they were disposed of.
(13) A judgment holder must keep a record referred to in subrule (12) as it relates
to abandoned goods for at least 3 years after the goods were returned, sold or
disposed of, as the case may be.
(14) The Court may make an order contrary to or varying any provision of this
rule.¹
[Alta. Reg.124/10;31/12;62/13]

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.29(2)
Related Provisions
3.7 (venue of enforcement) ; 5.21 (appointment for questioning) ; 5.28 (written questions) ;

6.18 (appointment for questioning) ; 6.40 (order to attend) ; 10.52 (contempt).
This Rule provides that any person can be examined (questioned) in aid of enforcement under
a court order. The debtor can also be required to swear a Financial Statement of Debtor.¹
The
stays under the Bankruptcy Act were held to have very limited effect on rights of examination
in aid of execution, or on proceedings to force answers to questions already asked at such an
examination.²
A debtor does not escape contempt for having failed to attend an examination in aid of
execution, by the fact that his lawyer was tied up or busy. Nor did earlier orders giving deadlines
exhaust the effects of the earlier breach of order. Since contempt is a ﬁnal order and hearsay is
not admissible, that applies to a medical or other excuse for not attending, and hearsay evidence
is inadmissible.³
There is no bar to ordering a Form 14 statutory declaration (revealing assets in lieu of an
examination in aid of execution), just because the debtor has a pending application to stay
enforcement of the judgment. Applying for a stay is not the same as getting it. Filing the Form 14
may be an inconvenience, but no more.⁴
On an examination in aid of enforcement,⁵
the witness must answer all relevant and material
questions. The witness need only inform himself from individuals over whom he has control.⁶
Credibility ﬁndings were made against a witness whose statutory declaration contained many
errors, and seeming deliberate obfuscation, such as repeated use of “n/a”. And he had access
to legal advice. This type of declaration must cover the topics which the Regulation requires; the
witness cannot choose his own topics.⁷
Costs were ordered against a self-represented litigant who tried to mislead the court and gave
very odd unsworn excuses for not obeying a court order, and had delayed throughout; but he got
two weeks more to deliver an “in aid” statutory declaration long overdue.⁸
A judgment debtor is required by the Civil Enforcement Regulation to provide detailed
employment information. If someone else, such as the union, holds that information, the individual
debtor could not “reasonably be expected to withhold consent” to having the union disclose that
information. The intent of the Privacy Act could not have been to frustrate enforcement of a
judgment. The Act is intended to protect reasonable and legitimate expectations, not illegitimate
ones.⁹

1 See ss.35.10 and 14 in Schedule 4 of the Civil Enforcement Reg. , AR 276/95.
2 Turkawski v.738675 Alta. (#12) 2007 ABQB 745, 435 AR 162.
3 Beaver Hills Hldg. v. Greenstreet Dev. Corp.2013 ABQB 203, [2013] AR Uned 255.
4 Beaumont Enr. v. Amik Oilﬁeld Equip. etc.2017 ABCA 327, Calg 170 0240 AC (one JA Oct 10).
5 On the subject of examination in aid of enforcement, note generally Abrams and McGuinness, Canadian

R.9.30 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
DIVISION 5
FORECLOSURE ACTIONS
When Afﬁdavit of Value Must be Filed
9.30 Unless the Court otherwise orders, an afﬁdavit of value must be ﬁled before
an application is ﬁled for
(a) a redemption order,
(b) an order that secured property be offered for sale,
(c) a foreclosure order,
(d) an order conﬁrming sale to the plaintiff or another person, or
(e) an order of possession, but not a preservation order.¹

Related Provisions
3.4 (venue) ; 3.41 (default of defence in foreclosure) ; 3.77 (subsequent encumbrances not

to be parties); 6.27 (preservation orders) ; 6.6 (notice of application in foreclosure) ; 11.22
(service of foreclosure documents) ; 11.23 (notice of address in foreclosure actions)
A. Introduction
There is a general discussion of the nature of a foreclosure action.²
Many parts of the Rules of Court do not fully codify the practice, but merely modify some
of the practices set by the old law. That is especially true of foreclosing on land mortgages and
agreements for sale. The bulk of the law in the foreclosure area is statutory, though equity forms
the foundation. Most of the statute law is designed to help the debtor.³
In Alberta, the statutory
layer is mostly created by the Law of Property Act,⁴
plus a few sections in the Land Titles Act.⁵
The courts of equity would allow a mortgagor or purchaser who had not paid on time, extra
time to redeem the land or perform the agreement for sale after the time set by the contract (and
by the courts of law) had expired. Foreclosure consisted of a court of equity giving him or her
more time to do so, and then ﬁnally ending his or her right to redeem. Statute sometimes ﬁxes or
presumes a redemption period.⁶

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous R.687. It came from 1968 R.686(7). Cf. C. R.1056 enacted by Alta. Reg.438/59,
amended Alta. Reg.172/62.
2 Daniels v. Mitchell 2005 ABCA 271, 371 AR 298 (¶’s 20 ff. ).
3 See Agric. Finl. Serv. Corp. v. Redmond (#2) (CA) [1999] 9 WWR 474, 71 Alta LR(3d) 189.
4 RSA 2000 c. L-7 Part 5.
5 See Price and Trussler, Mortgage Actions in Alberta (1985), and Price, Conducting a Foreclosure Action
(1996), especially pp.2-5, which gave ﬂow charts of the steps to follow and their order. These are somewhat
like critical path diagrams.
6 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 78, Part H. On changing the time to redeem, see W. P. I. G. v. Friesen (M) 2010
ABQB 155, 498 AR 398.

9-75
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.30
B. Alberta Practices
The two main Alberta statutory changes to the traditional Chancery practice are as follows.
First, in many circumstances an extra step is inserted after the period of redemption: the land
is offered to the public by way of judicial sale by tender. And second, sometimes the creditor is
restricted to the land and cannot get a personal judgment against the debtor. Much of Part 5 of
the Law of Property Act says when there is or is not that right. Section 41 of the Law of Property
Act does not extinguish the debt, nor bar other enforcement of it, such as foreclosure; it only bars
a personal judgment.¹
But not all contracts to sell land are “agreements for sale” which the Law of Property Act
requires go through foreclosure. A contract which is supposed to close quickly and leave nothing
owing, is not such an “agreement for sale”.²
A terrible trap for a mortgagee of a farmer, is the federal Farm Debt Mediation Act.³
The
Criminal Code now puts some limits on the interest rate which may be charged.⁴
There is no reason that the ordinary 2-year limitation period should not apply to any breach
of, or foreclosure suit on, a land mortgage. But giving a cheque, even a NSF one, could be an
acknowledgment re-starting time running.⁵
C. Procedure
An action for foreclosure is commenced by statement of claim.⁶
Who are to be the parties to
the suit can be tricky at times.⁷
The mortgagee can sue the mortgagor, or the guarantor, or both,
and in either order. Even collecting arrears or taking judgment on the covenant may not create
merger or protect the guarantor, if the guarantee so provides, especially if the debt had not been
accelerated.⁸

1 Telford v. Holt [1987] 2 SCR 193, 78 NR 321, 350. On when one can preserve personal liability by taking
a promissory note, or collateral security, see Magnum Mtge. & Realty Corp. v. Cruickshank 2011 ABQB
744, [2011] AR Uned 777 (Nov 29). If the mortgagor is a party to waste, such as damaging the property
by allowing someone to grow marijuana in the house, then that is a separate tort with no immunity: MCAP
Service Corp. v. Chiang 2011 ABQB 477, 523 AR 223.

to waive it, may get no costs: Intec Hldg. v. Grisnich (#2) 2004 ABQB 43, 350 AR 284. A foreclosure action
was not summarily dismissed, despite the Act, because the defendants were not true farmers: Graybriar
Land Co. v. Kovacs 2006 ABQB 693, 405 AR 211.
4 See on utility late charges and mortgages is discussed, with a distinction between agreeing to illegal interest,
and actually receiving it (the latter not occurring if the debt is outstanding long enough) : C. P. E. , Chapter 78,

starts a foreclosure suit, subsequent encumbrancers can ordinarily rely on that suit, and need not start their
own suits within the limitations period: T. -D. Bank v. Letendre, infra, R.9.34n. The limitation period may bar
action on a mortgage by a deemed transferee of it who has paid it off. Part payment and acknowledgment of
debt does not suspend time, if the person so acting is not the mortgagee then and could not enforce it then:

6 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 78, Part I and U.

be arguable: Royal Tr. Corp. of Can. v. Lodge at Waterton L. (M) 2005 ABQB 775, 389 AR 391.

9-76
R.9.30 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
The general foreclosure practice is not to have every secured creditor sue separately. If there
is an existing foreclosure suit which will distribute the surplus, other secured creditors can wait
and rely on that. Therefore a separate proceeding is not “warrant[ed]”, and the Limitations Act’s
expiry does not bar them.¹
Rules 11.23 and 11.24 somewhat modify the usual rules for service and addresses for service.
Often the defendant will ﬁle a mere demand for notice, not a Statement of Defence, but where a
default judgment becomes an issue, the defendant may be allowed to substitute a Statement of
Defence (possibly on conditions).²
The court refused to open up a noting in default in a foreclosure action because the only
defences alleged by the defendant were hopeless, i. e. not reasonably arguable.³
If the defendant were to ﬁle a Statement of Defence, most of the usual procedure for a defended
suit would apply. But rarely does the defendant defend a foreclosure suit. The basic facts cannot
be in dispute, where the defendant ﬁled no defence, was noted in default, and has not even tried
to open up the noting.⁴
If the plaintiff in a foreclosure action is not moving the matter quickly enough, the defendant
has remedies.⁵
On competing appraisals, see R.9.32 n. On receivers, see the Higgerty case.⁶
D. Redemption
In many cases, the court has the power to vary the time during which the mortgagor may
redeem.⁷
For that purpose, or to decide whether the creditor plaintiff should be able to buy the
land (where a deﬁciency judgment is desired), the court must know the value of the land, and the
plaintiff creditor must ﬁle a sworn expert appraisal, called an afﬁdavit of value.⁸
If the defendant has
no equity left, the redemption period may be short.⁹
The proper grounds to lengthen (or shorten)
the redemption period where the mortgagor is an individual, are listed in the Nemeth case.¹⁰
If
there is equity in the land and it is being maintained by the debtor, and the defect is caused by
events outside his or her control, giving a uselessly-short redemption period is wrong, and should
not be sought by a boilerplate afﬁdavit.¹¹
Some history is also given in the Nemeth case.¹²

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.31
A second mortgagee has the right to redeem the ﬁrst mortgage if it is in default, and so the
second mortgagee has a right to learn the balance under the ﬁrst mortgage, and it could sue to
get that, so there is no point to making it complicated to get that balance.¹
So a general privacy
legislation does not bar one mortgagee from telling how much it would cost to discharge the
mortgage) to another mortgagee of the same property; the latter has a right to redeem that other
mortgage. Compliance with a court order, is a statutory exception to privacy. There is implied
consent by the mortgagor (debtor). The privacy legislation does not cut down courts’ powers.
Another mortgagee is not a stranger to the court proceeding.²
A mortgagee can foreclose the interest of one co-mortgagor and leave the remaining co-
mortgagor’s interest on title (the two co-mortgagors being joint tenants) if the mortgage says that
they covenant jointly and severally.³
On a second mortgagee getting a Rice order to buy a deﬁciency judgment, and then claims
for indemnity under implied covenants, see the Carnahan case.⁴

ﬁle

(b) if the secured property includes chattels, the result of a current
Personal Property Registry search of each of the names of the
registered owners of the secured land, or the purchasers of the
secured land in the case of an agreement for sale.⁵

If a second mortgagee forecloses by taking title by way of order for sale, it is subject to the
implied covenant to indemnify the ﬁrst mortgagee who is ahead on the title, unless of course the
second mortgagee expressly excludes the implied covenant to do so.⁶
Offer for Sale of Secured Property
9.32(1) The Court may offer secured property for sale at a time and place, in a
manner and at a price that the Court considers appropriate.
(2) If the Court orders that secured property be listed with a realtor, a listing
agreement approved by the Court must, unless the Court otherwise orders, be
appended to, and forms part of, the order granted.⁷

R.9.32(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS

Related Provisions
6.64 (sale of personal property) ; 13.50 (clerk to hold tendered funds).

On conﬂicting evidence of value, see R.9.33 n.
The history of judicial sales of mortgaged land is discussed in the Zinck case.¹
The key document in a foreclosure suit is the so-called “order nisi/sale”. In some cases statute
lets one bypass part of that stage. Otherwise, it has these features:
1. Despite its name, it is a judgment, not a mere order, so that one cannot use hearsay
evidence (information and belief) to get it, and it creates res judicata.²
2. It ﬁxes the amount owing, which is the deﬁnitive amount to redeem, so no payout
statement is necessary.³
3. The application for it is (an) application for summary judgment, and so the principles
applicable to summary judgment presumably apply, unless speciﬁcally modiﬁed by the
foreclosure Rules or the Law of Property Act.⁴
4. It is called an “order nisi” because “nisi” is Latin for unless. It usually directs that the
land is to be offered for public sale unless the debtor sooner redeems (or undoes) the
acceleration by bringing his payments into good standing under the Law of Property Act,
if the ﬁnal due date is not yet here.⁵
5. On whether one needs to give notice of the application for the order nisi/sale if the
defendant has been noted in default, see R.6.5(2).⁶
Pre-1996 law must be approached
with some caution.⁷
6. After an order nisi of foreclosure, the mortgagee cannot exercise a contractual power of
sale without court approval, and the order nisi gives the mortgagor a power to redeem.⁸
The mortgagor has a statutory power to bring his or her payments up-to-date and so end the
acceleration which the mortgage created because of missed payments. If so, the plaintiff may get
a stay, and is not required to discontinue or have the order nisi set aside; if there is a new default,
the suit and order nisi may be revived.⁹
