Bank v. Poon 2012 ABQB 606, [2012] AR Uned 704 (Oct 2). An order/nisi of foreclosure creates issue
estoppel forever as to the amounts, and a party cannot later claim there are more, e. g. costs. If it reﬂects the
Applications Judge’s intention only appeal can change the amounts, however clear any error may be. R.9.12
cannot be used to correct it if the amount in it was deliberate. Some amounts are working out of the amount
in the order nisi and so can be added under R.9.14: AGF Tr. Co. v. Mcleod (M) 2011 ABQB 711, 527 AR
384. See the C. P. E. , Chapter 78, Parts T.4 and V.4.
3 Nat. Hldg. v. Goshko 2008 ABQB 465, 452 AR 394.

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.33(1)
Value of the land is important. But if two expert appraisals give very different ﬁgures, and
neither is cross-examined upon, ordering a short trial may be uneconomical, so a Master looked
at the comparable sales and their adjustments, thought one report had better comparables, and
used a weighted average between the two appraisals.¹
There is a general discussion of how judicial sales of land being foreclosed, are conducted
in Alberta.²
The court has some choice of means of judicial sale. At one time, an advertisement
in a newspaper was all that was done.³
But now usually realtors are retained or solicited with an
offer of a commission, a practice recognized by R.9.32(2).⁴
The sale is usually by sealed tender.⁵
The meaning and effects of a Rice order in foreclosure (on which see R.9.33 n. ) are recited.
The sale ordered binds the parties to it and extinguishes the mortgagor’s interest and equity of
redemption. The party who applied for and got the order must accept all of it.⁶
The court cannot
vary or cap the interest rate, even if the plaintiff moves its suit slowly in a falling market, but the
court could adjust the effective date of the Rice order sale to the plaintiff.⁷
A default judgment need not be set aside unless the defendant wants to contest a topic which
it decided. So an order nisi/sale may not rule on personal liability on the covenant, and if so it
need not be set aside to dispute that, especially if implicitly the in rem and in personam parts
of the suit were being separately dealt with. A late statement of defence limited to that topic may
then be allowed.⁸
Most of a new lawsuit complaining about the way in which a foreclosure action had been run
was summarily dismissed because of res judicata. In the previous suit there had been an order
for sale which was not appealed. In particular, claiming that the land had been sold too cheap is
a direct attack on the original order for sale.⁹
A mortgagee can foreclose the interest of one co-mortgagor and leave the remaining co-
mortgagor’s interest on title (the two co-mortgagors being joint tenants), if the mortgage says that
they covenant jointly and severally.¹⁰
Sale to Plaintiff
9.33(1) If a plaintiff seeks an order for sale of secured property to the plaintiff and
section 40(2) of the Law of Property Act does not apply, the Court must consider
whether a public sale should be attempted before the plaintiff’s application is
heard.

(2) The Court must consider at least the following factors:
(a) the nature of the secured property;
(b) the value of the secured property;
(c) the existing market for the secured property;
(d) the amount owed on the plaintiff’s security against the secured
property;
(e) the amount owed for prior charges against the secured property,
including prior encumbrances, municipal taxes and condominium
assessments.
(3) In granting an order for sale of the secured property to a plaintiff who has
made an offer or tender on the secured property, the Court must consider, in
addition to the factors in subrule (2), all offers or tenders made with respect to
the secured property.
(4) In granting an order for sale of the secured property to a plaintiff who has
not made an offer or tender on the secured property or whose tender has been
rejected, the Court must determine the fair value at which the plaintiff may
purchase the secured property.¹

Related Provisions
9.30 (affidavit of value)

The topic of personal liability for any deﬁciency if the sale of the land does not yield enough to
pay off the mortgage or agreement for sale, is a large one.²
Sometimes no stranger will offer a fair price for the land, and the court will let the plaintiff
creditor buy it for a fair price. That is called a Rice order.³
The plaintiff then usually pays no money,
and merely gives credit against the debt. That is done rather than ﬁnally foreclosing, so as to
preserve a deﬁciency if the defendant is personally liable. Some of the criteria for a Rice order
are now found in R.9.33.⁴
If the plaintiff seeks a Rice order but not a deﬁciency judgment, the
court can give a ﬁnal order of foreclosure instead. A chambers judge can weigh afﬁdavits which
conﬂict as to the value of the land.⁵
So can an Applications Judge.⁶
Where summary judgment is
not appropriate, there cannot be a Rice order. But if the property seems in jeopardy, a sale under
R.6.25(1) (c) may be proper. Where the plaintiff was a big trust company with a mortgage, it got

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.34(1)
the proceeds of sale on its undertaking to pay the defendant any damages which the latter might
ultimately be awarded, plus any excess of receipts over money found owing.¹
Generally a mortgage foreclosure order for sale to the plaintiff (Rice order) would need a
current appraisal, not merely a realtor market assessment. And if the market is falling, such an
order should be sought as promptly as reasonably possible.²
Though Crown agents (who are often lenders and so creditors) are not usually bound by
statute, they are bound by s.41 of the Law of Property Act when foreclosing, and so in some
circumstances they cannot get a deﬁciency judgment.³
On whether a second mortgagee buying becomes personally liable to the ﬁrst mortgagee, see
R.9.31n.
On belated efforts to appeal late an order in a foreclosure which the applicant had sought and
got, not realizing that it would bar a deﬁciency judgment, see the Demichele case.⁴
Where the mortgagee who was suing wanted to buy the property at market value, the court
would not adjourn the proceeding, as there was no evidence that the transferee numbered
company was making any payments or likely to do so. There was a small deﬁciency already, and
it would get bigger month after month. The transferee was simply stalling off proceedings while
collecting rent.⁵
On the subject of whether the value should be different on a forced sale, see CIBC Mtges. v.
Clarkson.⁶
A mortgagee wishing to make a bid should give only one price proposal. Allowing better offers
later would discourage everyone from making their best offers at the outset. Or would discourage
some people from bidding at all.⁷
Order Conﬁrming Sale
9.34(1) If the Court orders that secured property be offered for sale by tenders
ﬁled in Court and a tender is ﬁled, the plaintiff must apply for either or both of
the following:
(a) an order conﬁrming sale;
(b) an order rejecting tenders and directing the return of the deposits
paid in respect of the rejected tenders.
(2) The application must be scheduled for hearing not more than one month after
the date set for the receipt of tenders.
(3) On granting an order conﬁrming sale, the Court may provide any direction
that the Court considers appropriate to facilitate closing the sale.

A. Sale by Tender and Court Conﬁrmation
The sale is usually by sealed tender. The court does not have to accept any tender, and often
will not do so if none seems to be for enough money. Rule 9.34(2) sets time limits to apply to the
court either to conﬁrm a sale or reject the tenders and return the deposits.²
Though the court conducting a foreclosure sale has some ﬂexibility, it cannot impose terms on
an offeror (would-be purchaser) which were not in his tender and not agreed to by him.³
Conﬁrmation of an order for sale in a foreclosure suit irrevocably ends the equity of redemption.
So long as the conﬁrming order stands, there can be no redemption.⁴
A later bigger offer is not a ground to upset that conﬁrmation, especially as it would ultimately
upset the whole system of sale by tender.⁵
When a mortgagee seeks itself to buy the land (a Rice
order), it is impossible to sacriﬁce long-term encouragement of outside bidders by giving this
mortgagee a chance to match a slightly-higher outside bidder. The system should encourage the
mortgagee to make its best bid the ﬁrst time.⁶
The other parties must all get copies of all the evidence relied on by the applicant to seek
approval of the proposed sale. That is a matter of natural justice and fairness, and it is no excuse
that the procedure for sale in the previous order nisi had been followed, that learning the price
or land value could lead to a competing offer, or that the respondent to the application who was
not served would have failed on the merits, or that it could have found the information not served
some other way.⁷

9-83
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.34(4)
Much cold water is thrown on the notion of a forced sale for cash (in a foreclosure action),
especially when houses are selling readily. It should not be an arbitrary discount.¹
A defendant demanding production of documents after he was noted in default is too late,
especially if speciﬁc and about issues never pleaded.²
B. Closing the Sale
Now closing an approved judicial sale is usually done by the plaintiff’s solicitor, not the Clerk’s
ofﬁce.³
Complex issues arise with respect to tenants’ damage deposits, when there is judicial sale of
an apartment building or ofﬁce building. Deposits were not adjusted here.⁴
It is traditional for the plaintiff’s lawyer to pay any surplus into court, but to avoid the need for
later formal applications and afﬁdavits, it is better if the order nisi/sale instruct the Clerk what
to do with any surplus.⁵
If a judicial sale yields a surplus, the plaintiff’s lawyer must pay it all in
the way the order for sale directs, and cannot deduct other expenses, however reasonable, until
she gets court approval. Nor can she pay the plaintiff when the plaintiff does not have judgment
against all the co-owners.⁶
How to handle exemptions from enforcement for the debtor’s residence when dealing with a
surplus is discussed.⁷
A Master pointed out the complexities involved in claiming exemptions from
execution, and the time limit for doing that, where the funds in question are surplus funds left over
after paying the mortgagee in a mortgage foreclosure.⁸
C. Non-Parties
In Alberta, encumbrancers should not be made defendants unless they are in actual
possession of the property. Section 3(1) of the Limitations Act gives immunity only to a defendant
who pleaded the Act. The discoverability section speaks of knowing that something warrants
bringing a proceeding. Alberta practice was to allow a subsequent encumbrancer to ride the coat
tails of the plaintiff, so there was no reason for the encumbrancer to know that circumstances
warranted the encumbrancer bringing its own proceeding. The subsequent encumbrancer need
not show or prove that it had an enforceable claim independent of its charge registered on the
title.⁹
With or without the Civil Enforcement Act, a non-party claiming some interest in land cannot
pursue it in someone else’s foreclosure action against that land, without starting his own suit. A
foreclosure suit is not a proceeding to enforce a money judgment, and often no such judgment
is possible, and before such a judgment on the covenant is given, there is no money judgment to
enforce.¹⁰

R.9.35(1) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
D. Final Order of Foreclosure
If the plaintiff creditor is not seeking (or cannot get) a personal judgment, and is content to
take the land in ﬁnal satisfaction, after redemption and public sale have failed (or been bypassed
by statute), the creditor seeks a ﬁnal order of foreclosure. In the case of an agreement for sale, it
is called an order cancelling (or determining) the sale.¹
Checking Calculations: Assessment of Costs and Corrections
9.35(1) When in a foreclosure action the Court grants an order declaring the
balance owing to the plaintiff, an order for sale to the plaintiff or an order
conﬁrming sale, or grants judgment against any party, then, before entry of the
order,
(a) the plaintiff must, unless otherwise ordered by the Court, ﬁle and
serve on every defendant and subsequent encumbrancer any of the
following documents that are applicable:
(i) a statement of the secured indebtedness;
(ii) a statement of money received and disbursed in the sale;
(iii) a calculation of any deﬁciency, if a deﬁciency judgment or
declaration of a deﬁciency is ordered;
(iv) a bill of costs under rule 10.35 [Preparation of bill of costs];
(v) the proposed form of order,
together with a notice that any defendant and subsequent
encumbrancer who considers that any of the applicable documents
contains an error may ﬁle and serve on the plaintiff a written
submission before a date speciﬁed in the notice,
(b) the assessment ofﬁcer must assess the reasonable and proper costs
under rule 10.41 [Assessment officer’s decision] unless otherwise ordered by
the Court, and
(c) the assessment ofﬁcer must
(i) check the plaintiff’s calculations,
(ii) review any submission by a defendant or subsequent
encumbrancer,
(iii) correct the amounts in the order, if necessary,
(iv) sign the order as court clerk, and
(v) return to the plaintiff or the plaintiff’s lawyer the order and a
certiﬁcate under rule 10.43 [Certification of costs payable].
(2) When the order and the certiﬁcate have been returned to the plaintiff or the
plaintiff’s lawyer, the plaintiff may enter the order and ﬁle the certiﬁed bill of
costs.

A. General
Not all contracts to sell land are “agreements for sale” which the Law of Property Act requires
go through foreclosure. A contract which is supposed to close quickly and leave nothing owing, is
not such an “agreement for sale”.²
Where an issue in a foreclosure suit has been heard, and one side has not given any evidence
to rebut the opponent’s evidence, and the application is in effect for summary judgment, it is
appropriate to accept the sworn uncontradicted evidence to ﬁnd how much is owing.³
The redemption amount in a court order is res judicata, and cannot simply be “corrected”
later, though later lawful costs and charges permitted by the mortgage can be added.⁴
A ﬁnal order of foreclosure does not merge tort claims for deceit or fraudulent misrepresentation,
which are separate causes of action and not collateral attacks on the foreclosure order, still open
for a separate suit.⁵

9-86
R.9.35(5) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
B. Foreclosure Costs
1. General
There is a general discussion of foreclosure costs.¹
Usually a mortgagee is entitled to costs,
absent his misconduct.²
Costs are beneﬁcial and do more than indemnify: they produce more
efﬁcient and orderly justice and encourage settlement.³
An ordinary foreclosure action is not difﬁcult, and even involves a low risk of being sued
for negligence. Most work is done by paralegals ﬁlling out blanks in precedents and following
checklists in existence for years, not by actual work by lawyers. Challenging or complex legal
issues are rare. Even the lawyer who shows up in court to get the order does not have to do much
out of the ordinary. A third-year associate could readily do it. Taxation of even a solicitor-client bill
(called for by most mortgages) is easy and routine, and backup time records and so on are not
usually required.⁴
Southern Alberta has Guidelines for fees in a typical foreclosure, based on a survey of
prevailing fees.⁵
As mortgage insurer, C. M. H. C. is often involved in mortgage foreclosures and so
instituted a tariff of maximum legal fees which it would pay its insured lenders, and when lawyers
are retained by such insured lenders, they agree to that tariff. The Masters in turn adopted the
C. M. H. C. tariff. The tariff was instituted in 2004 and the Masters adjusted the amounts for inﬂation
in 2008. Then a 2015 revised Foreclosure Fee Guideline was issued by the Calgary Masters.
Two lenders approved it, but C. M. H. C. thinks those fees too high.⁶
The disbursement portions of
those guidelines are discussed in the CNR Inv. case on service,⁷
and the CIBC Mtges. case on
photocopies.⁸
In more recent years, lenders (trying to save money) have unloaded more duties on the
lawyers, who have tried to increase their fees accordingly. One duty is accepting payments on
account, as the mortgagees will not accept them. But $150 per payment is an excessive charge.
And now charges are being added for conveyancing after an order for sale. Generally $3, 850
would be all right.⁹
2. Contracts About Costs
Most mortgages and agreements for sale contain clauses which give the creditor solicitor-
client (full indemnity) costs against the debtor.¹⁰
Ordinarily they should be enforced.¹¹
They extend

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.35(5)
even to costs of a ﬁght about costs.¹
On appeal from a decision about whether the mortgagee
was guilty of vexatious or oppressive conduct and so is disentitled to such contractual costs,²
the
standard of review is deferential.³
Sec.40(1) of the Law of Property Act does not restrict the courts’
power to order costs and decide who will pay them.⁴
Solicitor-client costs are not recoverable if
the plaintiff sued too soon, e. g. after agreeing to accept a post-dated cheque for arrears.⁵
Nor if
the defendant was willing to pay the proper arrears, and the ﬁght was over improper charges?⁶
Despite the contractual costs in a mortgage, a mortgagee got only Schedule C costs against the
mortgagor, as he was largely an innocent dupe in a mortgage fraud which the bank could have
prevented by due care.⁷
The court has some discretion over costs even when there is a contract on the subject. The
court can disallow an award based on the contract, e. g. for unnecessary steps or extraordinary
measures.⁸
The steps claimed for must be necessary, in the sense of increasing the likelihood of
the lender’s security being protected and bringing the foreclosure action to an end. Inequitable
mortgagee conduct which would not be reimbursed would include misconduct, sharp practice,
and pursuing trivia in order to run up costs.⁹
A mortgagee is entitled to the solicitor-client costs called for by the mortgage, even for the
last motion which it lost (not being able to defend its ﬁnal order) where the mortgagee failed
only because the court accepted a new offer to purchase. The mortgagee was right to bring the
motion, and that was “exercising or enforcing or attempting to enforce” the ﬁnal order, within the
meaning of the costs clause. Whether someone did or did not get collateral beneﬁts from the
result does not change that.¹⁰
An Applications Judge cannot assess the reasonableness of fees or the work done to earn
them, even on a foreclosure suit.¹¹
Especially if there is a contract to pay the plaintiff’s legal bills.
The correct procedure is to have the assessment ofﬁcer review the fees.¹²
3. Procedure
Orders must set the level or type of costs: the assessment ofﬁcer cannot do that.¹³
Therefore,
they must be carefully worded, as it may be almost impossible to correct them.¹⁴
If contractual
terms as to costs are relied on, the contract must be in evidence.¹⁵
If a defendant noted in default

appears on the motion for sale, he is entitled to notice of assessment of the ensuing costs.¹
If

Rule 10.36 was applied even though not all the parties had consented. It was proved that no

party which had consented.³
In Southern Alberta, photocopies, postage, and courier charges are ordinarily set by the
Southern Alberta Foreclosure Costs Guidelines, which the assessment ofﬁcer should not change.⁴
There is a discussion of the procedure under R.9.35(4), which is not an appeal; it is a de
novo application with a fresh use of discretion. Time is important on foreclosure costs, but not
determinative, and records of time are important. This result tends to conﬁrm the range which the
assistant ofﬁcers have been following.⁵
C. Miscellaneous
There is a discussion of standard and acceptable orders (at different stages) in a foreclosure.
Template orders need notiﬁcation and checking to avoid internal contradictions.⁶
It was arguable that a mortgagor could claim back from the mortgagee the funds got by the
mortgagee on foreclosure, because the limitation period to sue on the mortgage had expired,
even though that limitation was not timely raised by the mortgagor.⁷
For Guidelines purposes, an application was “on notice” even though at the last minute the
respondent consented to the proposed order.⁸

and corrections] is served.

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.37
DIVISION 6
SALE AND DISPOSITION OF LAND OTHER
THAN BY FORECLOSURE ACTION
Application of This Division
9.37 This Division
(a) is subject to the Civil Enforcement Act, and
(b) does not apply to foreclosure actions.¹

Related Provisions
3.4 (venue) ; 9.32 (sale of land under foreclosure)

Sale and Disposition of Land
9.38(1) If land is to be sold, mortgaged, partitioned or exchanged as a result of
an action, the Court may make that order and specify the time and place of, the
manner of, and the price or sum associated with the transaction that the Court
considers appropriate.
(2) If the Court is satisﬁed that all interested parties are before the Court or
bound by the order, the Court may order
(a) the sale, mortgage, partition or exchange of land, and
(b) the procedure to be carried out to give effect to the order.
(3) Any money produced as a result of carrying out an order under this rule must
(a) be paid into Court,
(b) be paid to persons speciﬁed in the order, or
(c) otherwise be dealt with in accordance with the order.
(4) If a judgment or order states that land is to be sold,
(a) the sale must be approved by the Court before the sale is completed,
and
(b) the persons necessary to complete the sale must join the sale and
conveyance in accordance with the Court’s order.²
Information Note
Rules about payment into Court and out of Court are contained in Part 13 [Technical Rules]
Division 7 [Payment into Court and Payment out of Court].

If the property seems in jeopardy, a sale under R.6.25(1) (c) may be proper. The issues
between the parties should be tried. Where the proceeds go depends on the outcome.¹
After land was ordered sold to satisfy a writ of enforcement, later the court properly abrogated
the sale on the grounds that the terms of his earlier order setting the conditions of sale had not
substantially been met.²
A party wanted the court to return its deposit despite not closing a land purchase, a judicial
public sale under Court order. It had earlier got an extension of the time to close, on conditions
which it agreed to accept. The integrity of the court sale process is not decisive, but is important.
The offer was unconditional, and should stay that way. If the buyer did not have its ﬁnancial affairs
in order before it offered, that is its doing. The deposit was forfeit to the receiver and to the realtor.³
Terms, Conditions and Limitations on Orders
9.39 In an order under this Division the Court may include one or more of the
following terms, conditions or directions:
(a) that a person pay or account for rent or proﬁt, or both, to another
person;
(b) the manner in which the transaction is to be carried out;
(c) the person or persons who are to carry out or facilitate compliance
with the order;
(d) that any proceeds of the transaction be paid into Court or otherwise
paid to or disposed of by the Court.

Afﬁdavit in Support of Application for Order to Register a Convention Judgment
9.43(1) An originating application for an order to register a convention judgment
must be supported by an afﬁdavit
(a) stating that the applicant is entitled to enforce the convention
judgment and, where applicable, providing full particulars of the
entitlement, whether by assignment or otherwise,
(b) stating in the original currency of the convention judgment
(i) the unpaid balance and interest as of a ﬁxed date that is within

Footnote
2 months before the date of the originating application, and
(ii) the daily interest payable after that ﬁxed date,
(c) if applicable, stating the provisions of the convention judgment that
are sought to be registered,
(d) stating the manner in which the convention judgment debtor
submitted to the jurisdiction of the original court, if the convention
judgment debtor appeared, defended, attorned or otherwise
submitted to the jurisdiction of the original court,
(e) listing the reasonable costs of and incidental to registration and
certifying that the applicant has incurred, is liable to pay or is
otherwise entitled to collect them, as the case may be, and
(f) containing or to which is attached any other information or document
that the Court may require.
(2) The afﬁdavit referred to in subrule (1) must have attached to it
(a) a certiﬁed copy of the convention judgment and, where applicable,
a translation into English authenticated by afﬁdavit or any other
method acceptable to the Court, and
(b) proof acceptable to the court that the initiating documents were
served on the convention judgment debtor in the original application,
if the convention judgment debtor was served personally with the
originating documents in the original action, unless this information
appears in the convention judgment.
(3) An afﬁdavit may contain statements made on the information and belief of
the person swearing the afﬁdavit, but must state the source and grounds for the
information and belief.¹

evidence, and the extra ones set by Rules and case law for afﬁdavits, e. g. requiring personal
knowledge for ﬁnal applications. Such personal knowledge can be seeing events, identifying
otherwise admissible records, relevant belief, or recounting otherwise admissible statements
others were heard to utter.²

R.9.50(2) PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
On appropriate forum and attorning to a jurisdiction, see the notes to R.9.52 (n. B.3), and R.
11.25. On bars to enforcing a foreign judgment here, and what are not bars, see R.9.52n.
If you are a defendant in a foreign lawsuit and do not have assets outside the province, you
should carefully consider whether you should ﬁght the lawsuit on its merits in the other jurisdiction,
or instead endeavour to keep it from coming across the border once judgment is obtained. It is
often impossible to do both.¹
Bringing a judgment across the border to be enforced here is simpliﬁed if it was obtained in
a reciprocating jurisdiction. In that case, the application is made under the applicable legislation,
and is ex parte.²
But even if it is ex parte, an Originating Application must be used.
The Act does not apply to all judgments, so this question should be addressed ﬁrst.³
Registration of a foreign judgment is only a shortcut, and in no way bars suing on it at common
law, nor is it an excuse (under limitations law) for not so suing.⁴
If the defendant was not personally
served and did not defend, then an Originating Application is necessary to proceed under the
Act.⁵
If one proceeds outside the Act, a statement of claim is needed. If at the time of the foreign
suit the defendant did not reside in that state or province, nor carry on business there, and never
submitted to the foreign court, the Act does not let the foreign judgment be registered here.⁶
At
best, one would have to sue on the judgment.
A tribunal’s order ﬁled with another province’s superior court and so by their statute enforceable
as a court judgment, can be registered here. (There is special reciprocal enforcement legislation
for this topic. )⁷
There is no constitutional bar to enforcing in one province a judgment given in another (where
there was full attendance and attornment).⁸
The Dependent Adults Act⁹
allowed for resealing here of a guardianship or trusteeship order
from another province or territory, or a jurisdiction outside of Canada approved by the Registrar.¹⁰
Registration here under the Interjurisdictional Support Orders Act (or its predecessor, the
Reciprocal Enforcement of Maintenance Orders Act) does not make the “foreign” order an Alberta
order, and so an Alberta judge can vary it only to the extent expressly permitted by this legislation.¹¹

9.52 An application to set aside the registration of a judgment must be made by
originating application.³

Related Provisions
3.8 (originating applications)

A. Under the Legislation
1. Introduction
An application to have registration of the foreign judgment set aside may be made by
application. This is a fairly technical area. It is well to remember that only objection to registration
of the judgment can be taken. A defence to the original action on its merits is no longer available.⁴
In this regard, some avenues of defence to consider are:
(i) jurisdiction of the foreign court;⁵

Footnote
1 Karaha Bodas Co. v. Perusahaan Pertambangan etc.2007 ABQB 616, 435 AR 58.
2 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.734. It came from O. C.462-29 r.6 (ﬁrst two sentences), and was same as
1944 C. R.849 as amended by Alta. Reg.2/60.

5 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 61, Parts M and N. A court elsewhere has no jurisdiction to decide title to land
here, whether directly or indirectly via an in personam judgment on a contract of sale of land, and so such a

R.9.52 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
(ii) public policy;¹
(iii) fraud;²
(iv) whether, for example the foreign judgment is “ﬁnal, ”³
and
(v) time limits to move to set aside the registration.⁴
In this age of increasing interjurisdictional business, however, the days for resisting registration
of a judgment on some of these grounds may be numbered.
And of course one can register a judgment from elsewhere only if that province or state is
named as a reciprocating jurisdiction in an Alberta Regulation; otherwise one has to sue on the
judgment at common law (on which see Part B below).⁵
2. Times
For the Reciprocal Enforcement of Judgments Act, one tests the defendant’s province of
residence or place of carrying on business at the time the suit in the other province was begun.
Registration was set aside because the defendant was not ordinarily resident there nor served
there.⁶
3. Evidence
Where a defendant denies that he was carrying on business in another province and so denies
that its judgment can be enforced here, the party seeking to enforce the judgment has to give at
least some evidence that the defendant was carrying on business in that other province.⁷
“Carrying
on business” requires some actual direct or indirect presence in the province of judgment, plus
a degree of business activity sustained for a period of time.⁸
The presence must be actual not
virtual, either keeping an office there or regularly visiting that other province. Advertising there or
an internet presence is not enough.⁹
Being a shareholder or director of a company carrying on

1 Only fair procedure is necessary for enforcement: Dingwall v. Yang (Dornan) 2014 ABCA 89, 572 AR 106

question: id. at ¶’s 26-31. See the C. P. E. , Chapter 61, Part R. See further Part B below.
2 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 61, Part F, and Powell v. Cockburn [1977] 2 SCR 218, 8 NR 215, 225-31. Fraud
must be clearly proven: id. at 231-32(NR). The fraud may be as to where the defendant resides: ibid. Fraud
is relevant only if not adjudicated on by the foreign court and not discoverable by due diligence. Excessive
size of judgment does not demonstrate fraud. Natural justice is conﬁned to unfair procedure and does not
apply to merits of the suit. Public policy is relevant only if there is a violation of basic morality which shocks
the conscience of the reasonable Canadian. Excessive damages do not do that: ibid. Even intimidation must
be raised earlier with due diligence: Minnesota V. Alfalfa Prod. Coop. v. Baloun 2006 ABCA 210, 391 AR
257. That the foreign creditor had not ﬁrst realized on its security, is no bar to enforcing the foreign judgment,

AR 220. If a pending appeal abroad and an appeal in Alberta have different timing, one stay pending appeal
may have to be extended: Miller v. Magellan Morada Inv. Ltd. P’ship.2007 ABCA 328 (one JA).

PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.52
business in the judgment province is not carrying on business there.¹
Carrying on business is a
question of fact.²
4. Bouncing Enforcement
Can a two-step enforcement work? A foreign judgment, a second judgment elsewhere to
enforce it, and then a third judgment in a third place, to enforce the second one? The majority
declined to decide that issue, but Côté J. (concurring in the result) felt that that two-step process
was proper, and ﬁt within the legislation.³
A creditor got a judgment outside Canada (which the Privy Council upheld). The creditor
then sued the same defendant in British Columbia to enforce that judgment. No defence or
appearance being, a default judgment was obtained. The creditor then tried to register the British
Columbia default judgment in Ontario, which by local legislation reciprocates enforcement with
British Columbia. The legislation makes doing business there a precondition to registration, and
the judgment debtor was not carrying on business, nor ordinarily resident, in British Columbia.
Common-law terms are presumed to keep their usual meaning when used in legislation. Canadian
law on recognizing foreign judgments has now got wider bases (real and substantial connection),
but the legislation is based on the older common-law ones. Registration is just one optional way
to enforce a foreign judgment. Carrying on business in a province requires some direct or indirect
presence there, and virtual presence is not enough. In the context of the traditional enforcement
(registration) legislation “carrying on business” is stricter than carrying on business under the new
broader basis for court jurisdiction.⁴
No longer is service within the province of suit needed; now service ex juris. sufﬁces if the
province of suit had a real and substantial connection with the litigants or subject matter of the
suit.⁵
5. Procedure
Who are the proper parties to registration or enforcement proceedings is a question of Alberta
law, not foreign law.⁶
Where this legislation calls for “notice”, a warning with enough particulars is enough; the
Certiﬁcate need not be served.⁷
A Texas judgment registered in Quebec was in turn ordered
registered in Alberta. After failure to observe the Hague Convention on service was applied to a
different suit in Alberta with service in the U. S. A. , R.11.27 was amended, so now subrules (1) to
(3) apply despite that Hague Convention. So an order for service on a party’s Texas lawyers does
not bar recognition of the resulting judgment as a breach of natural justice.⁸

R.9.52 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
A judge should not have immediately proceeded to (in effect) respect North Dakota proceedings
over the same matters, without ﬁrst deciding issues of jurisdiction and proper forum.¹
Usually judgments from other provinces are ﬁled here without notice to the defendant, who
can then apply to have registration set aside. A plaintiff (judgment creditor) applied on notice
for registration here. The defendant (judgment debtor) had the onus of proving two points of
jurisdiction: whether the defendant was carrying on business in Manitoba, and whether the
defendant had been duly served with the Manitoba statement of claim.²
B. The Common Law
Usually a common-law remedy is older and statutory law is newer, but here the reverse is
closer to the truth. The legislation (in Part A above) mirrors parts of the former common-law,
whereas the current common-law (in this Part B) has been heavily modiﬁed by the Supreme Court
of Canada in the last 30 or so years.
1. Merits No Longer Relevant
Where the foreign court had jurisdiction or the defendant took part in the suit on the merits, our
local court cannot refuse to follow the foreign judgment because it seems mistaken, or because
of defences on the merits.³
Even if the foreign court originally lacked jurisdiction, attornment to it
by taking part in the suit on the merits gives the foreign court jurisdiction and allows enforcement
here.⁴
2. Public Policy
In rare cases public policy will allow our courts to refuse to enforce a foreign judgment
made within jurisdiction. The tests for features against public policy are usually strict.⁵
Though
enforcement here of a foreign judgment can be barred by public policy, requiring a company to
use a lawyer and not one of its lay ofﬁcers is not a breach of public policy.⁶
When public policy
is supposedly breached, usually that argument is based on breach of natural justice. But lack of
notice has to be very serious.⁷
An attack on foreign law does not make a foreign judgment against
public policy. A higher interest rate than in Canada is not enough.⁸

Footnote
1 Doell v. Doell 2013 ABCA 432, 566 AR 200.
2 Mt. Morgan Res. v. Guardian Helicopters, supra (¶’s 6 ff. ) (describing what is inadequate conclusory
second-hand information).
3 SHN Grundstuecksverwaltungsges. MBH & Co. v. Hanne 2011 ABCA 185, 505 AR 364; SHN

that public policy depends directly only on the result in the foreign court’s judgment, not directly on differing

9-101
PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS R.9.52
3. Jurisdiction
A foreign judgment tendered here either needs to be based on a real and substantial connection
of that foreign court with the litigants or with the subject matter of the dispute, or to be based on
the traditional grounds of jurisdiction. There need not be any real and substantial connection with
the province where it is sought to enforce that foreign judgment. Enforcement is limited to what
can be done within the conﬁnes of the jurisdiction of the province where it is sought to enforce
the foreign judgment.¹
All this recognition of foreign judgments is based on comity and respect
for the legal action of other states. Policy and fairness are regulated simply by seeking a real and
substantial connection with the country which gave the original judgment.²
Texas court decisions under U. S. federal bankruptcy legislation were recognized and enforced
in Alberta, using common-law rules of real and substantial connection with either the subject-
matter of the dispute, or with the defendants. Traditional elements such as attornment, agreement
to attorn, and residence or presence in the foreign state, also bolster real and substantial
connection.³
To enforce, comity is needed, and it can be found for even non-monetary judgments,
listing six relevant factors to weigh.⁴
Fraud, lack of natural justice, and public policy can be defences
to enforcement, but they are narrowly applied. Fraud must be fraud to get the judgment, not fraud
as an issue in the foreign trial. Natural justice is about ﬂaws in procedure, not in the merits of the
case. Public policy must be about the foreign law, not the fact ﬁndings (summary proceedings
being proper), or about a foreign court proved to be corrupt or biased.⁵
Real and substantial connection is the usual test both for forum non conveniens (on which
see R.11.25n. C), and for common-law recognition of judgments from other jurisdictions.⁶
The
Supreme Court of Canada tightened the tests for that in 2012.⁷
But the modern cases also
make reference to legitimate juridical advantage.⁸
The real and substantial connection test is
not conﬁned to judgments from within Canada, and also applies to recognizing truly foreign
judgments.⁹
A contract made in Florida to sell Florida land has a real and substantial connection
with the Florida courts.¹⁰
But substantial connection has far less weight if the parties contracted to
give that foreign court jurisdiction.¹¹
Where the Alberta courts only had jurisdiction to try to enforce
a foreign judgment (arbitral award) against assets in Alberta, and otherwise claimed the other
jurisdictions were the appropriate forum, then Alberta should not go into the merits of the validity
of the original judgment once the proceedings in Alberta have become moot, even in an attempt
to inﬂuence the courts in the jurisdictions that are the appropriate forum, to reconsider their earlier
decisions.¹²

9-102
R.9.52 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
On what is enough activity in a foreign jurisdiction to constitute doing business there and give
their courts jurisdiction and permit recognition here of their judgments, see the Wilson case.¹
Supreme Court of Canada dicta say that the courts should be more ﬂexible about enforcing
foreign non-money judgments. They must be ﬁnal, their nature must be such that comity requires
enforcement, and if they are equitable then the discretion whether to enforce them can be
exercised by the Canadian court. They should not be enforced more than would domestic orders.²
Even if the foreign court originally did not have enough connection with the suit, attornment by
the defendant will give it connection and make the judgment enforceable. A defendant who ﬁles
nothing with that court and merely asks the plaintiff for more time and states his (the defendant’s)
intent, does not attorn.³
The equivalent of a statement of defence is attorning to the jurisdiction,
and not opposing a summary judgment motion does not make the judgment a default judgment?⁴
But the mere fact that a foreign defendant ﬁled a statement of defence is not necessarily an
attornment to the Alberta court’s jurisdiction, and here it was not, because the defence ﬁled in
substance contested jurisdiction: it denied jurisdiction, and asserted forum non conveniens.⁵
Much
the same was true of the defendant’s one personal appearance before the court (by television
hookup). She never did anything else in the suit. Under the equivalent parts of the Quebec Civil
Code, ﬁling in the foreign court a motion to dismiss the lawsuit, part of which is based on the
substantive merits (and part on lack of jurisdiction) attorns to the foreign court’s jurisdiction, and
creates a substantive connection with the foreign court, and so the resulting foreign judgment can
be recognized and enforced in Quebec.⁶
As long as there is a “substantial” connection to the Province of Quebec, its securities ﬁnance
tribunal can regulate or discipline a trader or issuer outside Quebec. The Securities Commission
acts in the public interest, not strictly in a personal capacity. It is regulatory, not punitive or
restorative. It is not like ordinary private civil suits, e. g. in debt. The speciﬁc ﬁnancial legislation
need not give jurisdiction over those outside Quebec, nor limit the territorial reach of the tribunal.
The test is “real and substantial connection”, which looks at the degree of interconnection among
the legislation, its subject, and the person to be regulated. That test is for legislation’s constitutional
applicability, not for its constitutionality. So it interprets broad provincial legislation by limiting or
reading down its territorial reach. This is not the same as the “real and substantial connection” test
used elsewhere in conﬂicts!⁷
4. Penal Orders
Contempt is quasi-criminal, and our courts do not enforce foreign penal orders directly or
indirectly. The U. S. A. and Canada have different approaches to contempt, so there was a danger
that there could be inconsistency in remedies in the two countries.⁸
But indirect effects of contempt
do not make the resulting judgment penal, and a suit in contract or tort is enforceable even if the
defendant’s statement of defence had been struck out for not complying with court orders, which

in effect makes it an enforceable default judgment.¹
Whatever the procedural steps, if the decision
in question awards damages assessed as per sworn evidence of the loss, that is not a penal
order, and can be enforced here.²
5. Family Law
A foreign child custody order (as between two unmarried parents), will be recognized if the
children were ordinarily resident in that state when the order was made.³
For an interpretation
of the Hague Convention on abducted children, which exists to enforce custody (not access),
and a discussion of what is wrongful retention of a child under the Hague Convention, see the
authorities cited.⁴
6. Insolvency
If a foreign bankruptcy court whose proceedings have already been recognized in Canada
by an order, asks a court here to make a further order to protect assets or creditors, the court
here should not act as a mere rubber stamp, especially if non-monetary orders against persons
other than the bankrupt himself are sought. Especially where the foreign order is injunctive in
nature. The necessary tests include meeting the three-part test in the RJR-Macdonald case. It is
signiﬁcant that the order approved does what a Canadian trustee in bankruptcy would do.⁵
Even if our superior courts can recognize and enforce foreign bankruptcy decrees, what our
superior courts can do to enforce them is limited by federal legislation on the Federal Court of
Canada and its in rem decrees over ships.⁶
7. Miscellaneous
An express interest award in a foreign judgment cannot be ignored or altered in a suit here on
that foreign judgment. That includes post-judgment interest.⁷
Despite competing foreign orders on recognition or not of an arbitration award, summary
judgment was given in Alberta to enforce an arbitration award which had been conﬁrmed by the
Supreme Court of the country giving it. There was no public policy reason barring recognition
here.⁸
But a foreign arbitration award will not be enforced against someone here who was not a
party to the contract to arbitrate and did not take part in the arbitration.⁹
Limitation periods can also
bar enforcement of foreign arbitration awards.¹⁰

9-104
R.9.52 PART 9: JUDGMENTS AND ORDERS
A province cannot legislate validly on matters insufﬁciently connected with that province, and
what is sufﬁcient connection depends on the topic, and the individual affected, the province,
and their interrelation. Real and substantial connection enough to give that province’s courts
jurisdiction may not sufﬁce for legislative jurisdiction. Ontario could not legislate when the insurer
did not do business there. It is not enough that the car lessee resided in Ontario and got no-fault
beneﬁts from a different Ontario insurer (the one for their own car).¹
In a strong situation, persisting in trying to have a foreign judgment enforced in a different
province may be restrained by an anti-suit injunction.²
On parallel proceedings to enforce a foreign judgment, and to sue on the original cause of
action, and whether to stay one of them, see the Waud decision.³

3 Waud v. Dawson-Dixon 2023 ABKB 158, JCC 2101 06419 (Mar 16).
